California Masonic Memorial Temple – San Francisco

i

RICHARD WAKEFIELD HOPPER
Grand Master
September 30, 2007 – September 28, 2008

ii

RICHARD WAKEFIELD HOPPER
Grand Master – 2007-2008
Richard Wakefield Hopper was Initiated, Passed and Raised in 1964 in
Las Palmas-Ponderosa Lodge No. 366 in Fresno and later affiliated with a plural
membership in Visalia-Mineral King Lodge No. 128 in Visalia, where he served
as Master in 1998.
A native Californian, Dick began his association with Freemasonry
through the Order of DeMolay. He is a Past Master Councilor and holds the
Degree of Chevalier and the Legion of Honor. He has received the Rainbow
Grand Cross of Color and currently serves as Chairman of the Grand Executive
Committee of Rainbow Girls in California.
He is also a member of Eastern Star in Hanford, the York Rite Bodies
in Visalia, the San Joaquin Valley Shrine Masons of Tehran Temple, the
National Sojourners and Heroes of ’76, and is a 32º KCCH member of the
Scottish Rite Bodies of Fresno.
In 2001, he was appointed Junior Grand Deacon by Grand Master C.
Ray Whitaker, and in 2002 was appointed by Grand Master Bill Holsinger to
serve the Masonic Homes of California as a Trustee. He was presented the
Hiram Award by his lodge in 2003. In October of 2004, Grand Master Howard
Kirkpatrick honored Dick with the Grand Master’s Youth Support Award.
Bro. Hopper is a graduate of California State University, Fresno, and is
a retired real estate appraiser who specialized in the valuation of income
producing properties and in providing valuation support for eminent domain
litigation. He holds the MAI designation as a Member of the Appraisal Institute
and formerly served on its National Governing Council.
Dick is active in his church and is a past chairman of its Board of
Deacons. He served the Rotary Club of Visalia as its president and is a Paul
Harris Fellow of the Rotary Foundation of Rotary International. Public school
support in the community resulted in his being named a life member of the
California Parent Teachers Association.
Dick and Judi met when he was active in DeMolay and she was an
active Rainbow Girl. They have been married for 43 happy years and live in
Visalia. They have one daughter, a son and daughter-in-law, and two
grandchildren.
Bro. Hopper was elected in September of 2007 to serve your Grand
Lodge as its Grand Master.

I, ALLAN L. CASALOU, Grand Secretary of the MW Grand Lodge of
Free and Accepted Masons of the State of California, do hereby certify that the
following pages contain a true and faithful transcript of the proceedings of that
body at its One Hundred Fifty-Ninth Annual Communication, commencing at
the City of San Francisco, Friday the Twenty-sixth of September, A.L. 6008 and
terminating on Sunday the Twenty-eighth of September, A.L. 6008.

I have hereunto appended my official signature and have affixed the seal of the
MW Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted Masons of the State of California,
at the office of the Grand Secretary, this First day of January, in the year of
Light, two thousand and nine.

Grand Secretary

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

3

ONE HUNDRED FIFTY-NINTH
ANNUAL COMMUNICATION
FRIDAY AFTERNOON, SEPTEMBER 26, 2008
The 159th Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge of Free and
Accepted Masons of the State of California was called to order by Grand Master
Richard Wakefield Hopper on Friday, September 26, 2008.
Worshipful John Crabtree, Master of Visalia Mineral King Lodge No.
128, raised the opening session of Grand Lodge.

Reception of the Past Grand Masters
The Grand Master welcomed the brethren and directed the Grand
Marshal to present the Past Grand Masters of the Grand Lodge of California at
the Altar. The Grand Marshal then introduced each Past Grand Master,
following which the Grand Master, accompanied by the Deputy Grand Master,
personally greeted each one. The Grand Master asked the brethren to join him
in saluting our Past Grand Masters by the battery of three times three. The Past
Grand Masters were then escorted to seats reserved for them in the auditorium.
The Grand Master then directed the Grand Secretary to send to each absent Past
Grand Master the usual letter of greeting from the Grand Lodge.

Roll Call
The Grand Secretary was asked to call the roll of Grand Lodge
Officers, Past Grand Masters, and Past Grand Officers, and to ascertain if a
constitutional number was present to convene the Grand Lodge of California.
The following were present as indicated by their response to the calling of the
roll:

The Grand Secretary announced that, according to the Credentials
Report, there were 658 Master Masons registered as of 12:00 p.m. Friday, with
at least twenty percent of the Lodges chartered by this Grand Lodge
represented, and that therefore a quorum was present as required for the
transaction of business.
The Grand Secretary reported that all Lodges have paid their annual
per capita assessment to Grand Lodge except for the following Lodges:

Grand Lodge Opening

The Grand Master then opened Grand Lodge in ample form. The
Grand Marshal and the Deacons retired to escort the Flag of the United States of
America into the Auditorium, which was followed by the Pledge of Allegiance.

Introductions
The following were asked to rise as groups for recognition: Hiram
Award Recipients, Diamond Jubilee Award Recipients, Golden Veteran Award
Recipients, Worshipful Masters, Lodge Secretaries, Grand Lodge Committee
members, members of Constitutional Boards, District Inspectors, and Visiting
Brethren from other jurisdictions. Each group was greeted with a round of
applause.

REDEDICATION CEREMONY
The Rededication Ceremony of the California Maosnic Memorial
Temple was held in the Masonic Auditorium of the California Masonic
Memorial Temple, 1111 California Street, San Francisco, California beginning
at 5:00 p.m.
Grand Master Richard Wakefield Hopper, accompanied by his Grand
Lodge Officers, entered the Auditorium to the sound of the organ, and assumed
their stations.
The Grand Master welcomed all in attendance to the Rededication
Ceremony and asked the Grand Chaplain, the Very Reverend Arthur H. Weiss,
to offer the invocation.
Grand Master Hopper then introduced the following guests: Mr. Steve
Kawa, Chief of Staff for the Honorable Gavin Newsome, Mayor of the City and
County of San Francisco; and the Very Reverend Alan Jones, Dean of Grace
Cathedral. Grand Master Hopper also introduced Brother Charles Williams, the
founder of Williams Sonoma, and a member of Temple Lodge No. 14 in
Sonoma. Williams Sonoma is also celebrating the 50th Anniversary of their first
store in San Francisco this year. The Grand Master then recognized as a group
those in attendance who were also present at the laying of the Cornerstone in
1956 or the Dedication of the Temple in 1958.

Rededication Ceremony
The Rededication Ceremony began with the Grand Secretary reading a
list of the items contained in the time capsule which was part of the original
laying of the Cornerstone in 1956. The contents of that time capsule were as
follows:

The Grand Treasurer was then instructed to deposit the capsule in its
proper place. Adam Kendall, Collections Manager for the Henry Wilson Coil,
Jr. Library and Museum then gave an explanation of the implements and their
significance.
After the completion of the Cornerstone Rededication Ceremony, the
Grand Orator was called upon to address those in attendance. His remarks may
be found in Appendix H.
Following the Grand Oration, the Grand Master called upon Mr. Steve
Kawa for his remarks. Mr. Kawa read a proclamation from the Mayor of San
Francisco proclaiming September 26, 2008 the California Masonic Memorial
Temple Day.
The Grand Master then invited Worshipful Bro. Richard Pierce, Most
Worshipful Bro. Bill Holsinger, and Very Worshipful Bro. John Cooper to
come forward to present flags which will henceforth fly over this building.
The Grand Master closed the ceremony by remarking that California
Masonry celebrated a milestone moment in its history by honoring the memory
of brothers gone before, and rededicating themselves to upholding the virtues
that they stood for and which unite brothers across time.
Robert Wilson, PM, Secretary of Naval Lodge No. 87 closed the
ceremony with a moving a cappella rendition of the Star Spangled Banner,
followed by the Benediction given by the Very Reverend Alan Jones, Dean of
Grace Catherdral.
The Grand Master thanked everyone for attending the 50th Anniversary
Celebration of the Californai Masonic Memorial Temple and invited all to a
light reception in the Lobby

SATURDAY MORNING, SEPTEMBER 27, 2008
The Grand Lodge convened at 8:30 a.m., and after prayer by the Grand
Chaplain, the Grand Master noted that Grand Lodge was raised by Worshipful
Gordon Keeton, Master of Las Palmas Ponderosa Lodge No. 366.

Introduction of the District Inspectors
The Grand Master then directed the Grand Marshal to escort into the
auditorium the District Inspectors. The Grand Master thanked the Inspectors for
their continued service to the Fraternity as the representative of the Grand
Master.

Grand Oration
The Grand Master then called upon the Grand Orator, Worshipful
Mark N. Gibson, who presented the Grand Oration (Appendix H).

Legislation
Grand Master Hopper then reminded the members of the established
rules and relinquished his chair to the Deputy Grand Master before presenting
his Decision and Recommendations.

Most Worshipful Frederick L. Sorsabal, Past Grand Master, delivered a
eulogy for the late Past Grand Master, Most Worshipful Robert O. West.

16

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

EULOGY
ROBERT O. WEST
Robert Orville West was the 116th Grand Master of Masons in
California and Hawaii. Bob, as he wanted to be called, was a man of service to
his community, our Nation and to our Fraternity. Most Worshipful Bob’s theme
for the 1981-1982 Grand Lodge Year was “Masonry: A Foundation for Man,
For Family, and for County”. His natural warmth and outgoing love
exemplified Bob in everything he did and touched.
Bob was born in Santa Cruz, California on August 10, 1926 to Harvey
F. and Leila E. West. The West family was well known in the Santa Cruz area
and, in fact, have a park named for Bob’s father in that city. Subsequently, the
family moved to Placerville where Bob started working in his father’s lumber
mill at the age of 14 and learned every aspect of the lumber business becoming
the President and Sales Manager of Placerville Lumber Company in the heart of
California’s Mother Lode region. He and his Brother Harvey West, Jr. are
partners in the development of Graeagle Land and Water Company.
Most Worshipful Bob graduated from El Dorado High School where
he had gone to school with Jean his future wife. After high School, he enlist as
a Cadet in the Army Air Corps and later enlisted in the Air Force Division of the
Armed Forces. He served until the end of WW II.
Bob and Jean married in 1947 and were blessed with six children, 10
grandchildren and 2 great grandchildren. Their children were involved with the
Order of DeMolay and the International Order of Rainbow for Girls.
Bob petitioned to receive the Degrees of Masonry in El Dorado Lodge
#26 (now known as Placerville Lodge #26) and was raised to the sublime
Degree of Master Mason on October 28, 1954. Bob has told everyone that
several of the employees of his father’s lumber mill had encouraged him to
become a Mason and he found the teachings of Masonry to be fundamental and
would become instrumental to his life. He served as Master of his Lodge in
1959; Master of Northern Past Master’s Association in 1976; served on the
Grand Lodge Committee on Charters for fourteen (14) years; was a member of
the California Masonic Memorial Temple Board of Directors; and was
appointed Junior Grand Deacon by Most Worshipful Herbert Huebner, 19701971. Bob was elected Junior Grand Warden in 1978; Senior Grand Warden--1979; Deputy Grand Master---1980 and Elected Grand Master in 1981.
Bob enjoyed working with the Masonic Youth groups and became the
Chapter Dad of El Dorado Chapter, Order of DeMolay. He received the
DeMolay Honorary Legion of Honor for his work and was Past Dean of the
Sacramento Preceptory of the DeMolay Legion of Honor. He and his wife
worked with the Placerville Assembly of Rainbow for Girls.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

17

His membership in Concordant bodies included: the Scottish Rite
Bodies of Sacramento were he was coroneted an Inspector General Honorary of
the 33 rd Degree in 1987; Ben Ali Shrine; Order of Eastern Star; Royal Order of
Jesters; Ancient Egyptian Order of Sciots; York Rite Masons; Order of
Amaranth; and National Sojourners.
While serving as Grand Master, Bob implemented his policy of
“Masonry Out Front” by successfully implementing the publication and
distribution of a newspaper insert that told the Masonic story to non-Masons on
the occasion of George Washington’s 250th Birthday. Included in 4 million
editions of metropolitan newspapers throughout California and Hawaii, this
presentation accomplished the goal of telling the true story of Masonry to
multitudes who otherwise would have continued to believe the myths which the
ignorant and prejudiced have so long attempted to surround our Craft. The
reaction expressed within and without this Jurisdiction was extremely positive.
Also during his term as Grand Master, Bob was able to raise two of his sons to
the Sublime Degree of Master Mason in an outdoor mountain-top ceremony and
he dedicated and laid the Cornerstone of the Masonic Center in Placerville.
Most Worshipful West’s community affairs were enormous. His
attitude was that he should give more than he received from the community. To
generate as idea, espouse a cause, and see it through to fruition has been Bob’s
philosophy toward the interests and affairs of his community – participation
whose beginnings he attributes to the inspiration of Masonry. To this end, he
joined and actively participated in the Benevolent Protective Order of Elks;
American Legion; Rotary International where he served as President; El
Dorado County Boy Scouts, District Chairman, 1969-70 and holder of the Boy
Scout’s Silver Beaver Award; El Dorado Chamber of Commerce serving as
President; Marshall Hospital Board of Directors serving as President for 8 years;
Founder and President of the Marshall Hospital Foundation; American Heart
Association; El Dorado County Juvenile Justice Commission; President of Cold
Springs Country Club and holder of #1 club membership; was named El Dorado
County Outstanding Citizen in 1973; Board member for the Placerville Union
Cemetery; and designated as one of the 10 most influential citizens in El
Dorado County in 1980. Bob’s contributions of time, energy, and effort for his
community was guided by a sincere desire to do for others and he had an honest
joy in the doing, but no matter what the work, he has always done it as a Mason.
Warmth and humility have always pervaded his actions and his words.
His abiding faith in the triumph of Masonic ideals has been the touchstone of
his many accomplishments throughout the years, the result of leadership of love
and understanding. Most Worshipful Robert West personifies the qualities of
loyalty, affection, and dedication which are the cornerstone of true Brotherhood.
His example and life will continue to be a constant reminder and inspiration to
the Fraternity to which he has given so unselfishly.

18

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

We extend our condolences to his family and the many friends and
Brothers he leaves behind.
Go in Peace good friend.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

ROBERT O. WEST
PAST GRAND MASTER
August 10, 1926 – August 1, 2008

20

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

SAM M. PAVLOVICH
PAST GRAND MASTER
February 1, 1921 – April 16, 2008

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

21

Most Worshipful Vernon L. Gordon, Past Grand Master, delivered a
eulogy for the late Past Grand Master, Most Worshipful Sam M. Pavlovich.

EULOGY
SAM M. PAVLOVICH
My friends, we have met here to celebrate the life of our friend and
brother. But I ask each of you to contemplate your own station in life as you
hear these words.
I had walked life’s way with an easy tread
I had followed where comforts and pleasures led
Until one day in a quiet place
I met the Master face to face.
With station and rank and wealth for my goal
Much thought for my body, but none for my soul
I entered expecting to win life’s race
When I met my Master face to face.
I met Him and knew Him and blushed to see
That His eyes, full of sorrow, were fixed on me
I faltered and fell at His feet that day
While my castles melted and faded away.
Now my thoughts are for the souls of men
I have lost my life to find it again
Ever since that day in a quiet place
I met the Master face to face.
Our friend and companion with conscience clear
In the company of those whom he held dear
Is at rest in his new home a quiet place
He is with his Master face to face.
Our brother Sam was a first generation American from our great
central valley. He was the son of immigrant Serbian parents who settled in
Fresno after the First World War.
He springs from solid hard working stock. He was raised with
traditional old world values of dedication to family, church and community.
Sam Pavlovich exemplified those fine qualities which have built America.

22

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Born in Fresno on February 1, 1921, he as raised in a family of five
children, one brother and three sisters. Our friend and brother studied
engineering at Fresno State College. His studies, however, were abruptly
interrupted by the Second World War, when he enlisted in the US Navy. He
served aboard a destroyer in the European, Mediterranean and African theaters
of war. And while still on active duty he was sent to the California Institute of
Technology at Pasadena where he continued his studies until earning his
honorable discharge.
Our distinguished brother then completed a degree in civil engineering
and became a registered civil engineer. He held a certificate in transportation
planning from the University of California. He subsequently worked for the
California State Division of Highways and Cal-Trans for thirty-three years. His
district office was responsible for the planning, design, construction and
maintenance of the rapidly expanding highway system in the Central San
Joaquin Valley, including portions of US 99 and Interstate 5.
Our friend and brother Sam was for the most part a quiet and softspoken man and yet he was possessed with a deep and abiding faith in his own
convictions and his personal concept of right and wrong. He was not easily
swayed by mere public opinion or the changing winds of time. He was
possessed of the great virtue of being able to live in his private life those same
principles he displayed in public appearance.
As we pause here in the presence of God in the quiet of these hallowed
walls it’s a part of our nature to experience his loss, to mourn his absence, and
also to hold a deep and sincere sorrow of our own personal loss. The emptiness
that could be ours, however, should instead be filled with his never vanishing
friendship and his ever present smile.
His memory should be a part of our innermost thoughts at this moment,
for his work now becomes our work, and his devotion to purpose now becomes
an addendum to our own assignment with destiny.
It is my earnest hope that we not use this precious time lamenting the
things that we don’t have. It is true that we can no longer reach out and touch
Sam and listen as quietly as we might we will not hear his voice. But, with so
little effort we can dwell on the things we do have. The memories of his voice,
the pleasant conversations we have had with him, the bonds of genuine
friendships that we have established. These things will remain with us long
after the sunset of this day. These things are ours forever.
God saw Sam getting tired and a cure was not to be so He put His arm
around him and whispered come with me! With our prayers we supported him
yet to see him pass away and though we loved him dearly we could not make
him stay. A proud heart stopped beating, hard working hands at rest, God
yearned for a saintly builder and so He chose the best.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

23

And so, my friends, nature will have its way. Our tears will fall on his
coffin in sorrow for our own loss but our memory of Sam will not be erased or
forgotten. We will continue to see his image, his features, hear his voice,
possess his thoughts, and see his vision as we continue our relationships with
his friends and family.
It is because of men like Sam Pavlovich that we come to know and
appreciate truth and become worthy of its meaning. And so the truth is, not
because I say it, but because it is so, is that Sam was a valued friend. He was an
honest and upright citizen and he was worthy of all emulation.
It is God himself who determines the length of our lives but He
completely relies upon us to determine the depth of our lives. We could use
many words to describe the life story of our friend Sam. If you sum them all up,
in and of themselves, they could not begin to describe the depth of his life.
Instead of searching for the many words for terms most common,
perhaps we should examine just one. The word that comes to my mind,
strangely enough, is so simple and yet so powerful in its use that many grown
men have great difficulty saying it in public. The word is love. If there is a
message for us in the way Sam lived his life, it could be embodied in that word
as it dominates these words of scripture.
And now I beseech thee, not as though I wrote a new commandment
unto thee, but that which we had from the beginning, that we love one another.
And this is love, that we walk after his commandments. By this we know that
we walk in the truth. Behold what manner of love the Father hath bestowed
upon us, that we should be called the children of God, that our joy may be full.
Beloved, let us love one another for love is of God and those that loveth are
born of God and knoweth God. Herein is love, not that we loved God, but that
he loved us.
My friends, if Sam, through God loved us, can’t we at least tolerate one
another? We must, for the future of our society depends upon it.
It’s a part of our nature to join together on these occasions, and we do
join together as family, as friends of family, as fraternity. We join together, not
to mourn, but in remembrance, and to share in the joy of the bonding of old
friends. We join together to reflect upon the life of one who has gone from our
sight, and to remember what his presence meant to the lives that surround him.
Its difficult though, if not impossible, to remember each moment that
has been shared over the span of time, for our ability to recreate and to relive
times that were is limited by the shallow capacity of our human frailties.
Edwin Markham wrote: “At the heart of the cyclone tearing at the sky,
there is a place of central calm.” In the midst of the storm of death, our brother
has found a place of central calm. He has left all things quietly to God.

24

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

And so in these times when our eyes no longer behold what we have
loved, and when we listen for the footsteps of those who have gone out of our
sight, and hear them not, and when our hearts fail us and we sigh for that which
cannot be, and there are few to comfort us, we too, my friends should trust
quietly in God.

The Memorial Service concluded with a musical selection performed
by the Grand Organist and the Junior Grand Deacon.
At 11:55 a.m. the Grand Master called Grand Lodge from labor to
refreshment, to be called on again at 1:15 p.m.

Election of Officers
The Grand Master then asked the Grand Secretary to explain the
balloting procedure for the election of officers. No ballot would be necessary if
the office were not contested. If more than one nomination were received for an
office, ballot cards would be distributed for the purpose of balloting for that
office.

Presentation of the Membership Development Award
In the period of July 1, 2007 to June 30, 2008, our Lodges conferred
4,091 degrees. In 2003, we introduced the Pass It On program full of new
resources for Lodges and members to use in sharing Freemasonry with their
family, friends, and colleagues. As part of that program, a goal was set for each
Lodge to raise at least five new Master Masons a year. This year over 100
Lodges have accomplished that feat. Each of those Lodges will receive special
recognition from the Grand Lodge for their great work.
The Grand Master made a special presentation to Moreno Valley
Lodge No. 804 who during the last 12 months rasied a total of 20 Master
Masons.

Presentation of the Leadership Training Awards
The Lodge Management Certification Program consists of five courses
that are conducted simultaneously at a particular location or online. The courses
include:
Hall Association Management, Lodge Finance, Membership
Development, Program Planning, and Lodge Administration. Each course is a
full day in length. At the end of each course, participants must pass an
examination before they are given a certification in that course.
The Grand Master presented plaques to the following brothers who
have completed all five courses of the Lodge Management Certification
Program:
Edgardo Lopez
Criag A. Sanborn
David A. Coleman
Mark L. Hodges
Gerald L. Harbick
David L. Revels

Roy L. Youngblood

Ronald B. Ragland

Ritual Awards Presentation
“Ritual is an integral part of the foundation of Freemasonry. The great
lessons of the fraternity are found in and between the spoken words that we
deliver during our meetings and ceremonies. To promote high standards in
ritual presentation and performance, the Ritual Committee of the Grand Lodge
sponsors an annual Ritual competition. At his time, I call on our Grand
Lecturer, the Very Worshipful Paul Hennig, for the presentation of the Ritual
Competition awards.”
The following lodges won the Division Level competition:
Division I: Liberty Lodge No. 299
Division II: Bicentennial Daylight Lodge No. 830
Division III: Atwater Larchmont Tila Pass Lodge No. 614
Division IV: Irvine Valley Lodge No. 671
Division V: Central Coast Lodge No. 237

In addition to winning the Division Competition, Irvine Valley Lodge
No. 671 won the state-wide competition.

GRAND MASTER’S MESSAGE
I wish to extend my welcome to all who are in attendance with us this
morning, including not only the members of our Craft in California, but also
those of our Fraternity who are visiting us from out of state. It is a pleasure and
an honor to have you join with the leadership of Grand Lodge at this 2008
Annual Communication. I thank you for your presence.
I specifically want to take this time to thank my good friend and
Brother from Oklahoma, Most Worshipful Johnny Onkst, for his response to my
earlier welcome of the visiting Grand Masters, their representatives, and their
officers and brethren.
My friendship with Most Worshipful Johnny is tied to our mutual work
on the Masonic Renewal Committee of the Conference of Grand Masters of
Masons in North America. He is a tireless worker for Masonry in his
jurisdiction and across this land and I deeply respect him for his leadership and
high standards of conduct.
Section 404.130 of the California Masonic Code requires the Grand
Master to present a written report to the Craft at Annual Communication setting
forth all his official acts during the year, the general condition of Masonry
within this Jurisdiction, and recommended actions for legislation that he deems
necessary or expedient for the welfare of the Craft. I will now endeavor to do
that in this report.
Those of you who know me well know that I try to stay the course of
positive thinking. I have endeavored to govern with that state of mind this year.
I am a true believer that progress is most easily and effectually achieved
through personal one on one relationships and I have made the effort to develop
those relationships this year. Many of you have opened your mail or picked up
your telephone this year to find that the Grand Master was writing or calling you
directly. Thank you for being willing to talk to me; together, I believe that we
have grown and become better Masons through such contacts.
While this is the report of the Grand Master, it really reflects the
activities of innumerable of our Brethren from the elected men of the Executive
Committee all the way through the Craft in this jurisdiction including the
Committeemen, Grand Lecturer, Assistant Grand Lecturers, Inspectors,
Worshipful Masters, elected and appointed Lodge officers, and, most
importantly, the dedicated Masons who fill the seats on the sidelines of our
constituent Lodges on a regular weekly basis.
The Official Acts of your Grand Master this year are many in number
and varied in subject matter. I am relating them to you in this report.
Over the long history of the Grand Lodge of California, there have
been only eleven Grand Secretaries. The tenth Grand Secretary was John

46

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Lilburn Cooper III who served us with unwavering dedication for some eighteen
years. Very Worshipful John reached a milestone on April 1, 2008, when he
reached the age of 65 years and made the decision to retire. The consequence of
his retiring was the creation of a vacancy in the office of Grand Secretary. In
accordance with our Code, it became my duty to fill that vacancy and I did so.
John Cooper left many legacies in his pathway of Masonic life, but
none more valuable to the Craft than his selection and mentoring of his
successor over the past eight years. It was Very Worshipful John who
suggested to me that his Assistant Grand Secretary, Allan Leslie Casalou, was
not only a good choice to be his successor, but he was the only Brother trained
and qualified for the job. As Grand Master, it was my pleasure to appoint Very
Worshipful Allan into the position of Grand Secretary on the same day that I
accepted Bro. Cooper’s retirement papers. Since that time, Very Worshipful
Allan has been installed into the office, has taken the reins of management, and
is proving that he is, indeed, the most qualified man for the position.
The records of the Grand Secretary’s office include a full report of the
number of charters suspended and returned during the year and the number of
members who were suspended or expelled. Also from the Grand Lodge records,
I am reporting to you that the number of Masonic Trials occurring this year has
fluctuated from a low of six to a high of forty one. This number of trials is very
difficult to manage and, quite frankly, not reasonable. Many are the result of
personal conflicts between Brethren over issues that are not really Masonic in
nature and they should never progress to the trial state. As a result, I am
bringing Grand Master’s Decision No. 1 to you later in this Annual
Communication and it deals with Alternative Dispute Resolution and a
mediation process.
Although I remain positive, I am concerned that we continue to have
some “troubled Masons” in California; men who have forgotten the obligations
they took at our altar and who are causing disharmony among Brethren and
displaying fiscal irresponsibility, candidate hazing, ballot box abuse,
disrespectful language, and physical abuse and threats.
As I previously noted, relationships are extremely important to me and
I am pleased to report some statistics to you that are reflective of relationships
made between Lodges and Masons and your Executive Committee. This year,
your leadership has been physically on the level in 73 constituent lodges, on the
lodge Installation Teams in over 40 Lodges, performed 18 Cornerstone
Ceremonies dedicating schools and public buildings, attended 28 Concordant
Body events, and attended numerous local meetings of our Masonic Youth
Orders.
Members of your leadership team represented our California
membership at the Conference of Grand Masters of Masons in North America
held in Louisville, Kentucky and at the World Conference of Masonic Grand

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

47

Lodges in Washington, D.C.
They have also continued our positive
relationships with other jurisdictions including Washington, Oregon, Nevada,
Arizona, Hawaii, and Texas.
As your Grand Master, I was given the privilege of presenting two 75
year Diamond Veteran Awards and one certificate commemorating the 82 year
membership and regular lodge attendance of one of our own.
This evening, I will officiate at the Ceremony during which we will rededicate this California Masonic Memorial Temple. This magnificent edifice,
erected and first occupied in 1958, is now fifty years old and we intend to rededicate it to Masonic principles and truths and to have quite a birthday party.
Part of my job description as Grand Master dictates that I reach out to
those who may become future Brethren of our Fraternity or our Concordant
Bodies. I have done that and have experienced the vigor and vitality of our
youth. In Anaheim, California, I brought remarks to the International sessions
of the Order of DeMolay and encouraged our young men to consider our Craft
Lodges as they matriculate out of their Youth Order.
In Chicago, Illinois, I attended the biennial sessions of Supreme
Assembly of the International Order of Rainbow for Girls. Over the years, I
have seen many of young women of Rainbow encourage their fathers to join our
Craft and we have benefitted greatly from our sponsorship of such a wonderful
Youth Order.
In Madison, Wisconsin, I extended greetings from the Masons of
California to the assembled young ladies and adults of Job’s Daughters
International. In my remarks, I encouraged them to seek positive relationships
with their sponsoring Masonic Bodies and to stay grounded in the roots of
Freemasonry.
Also in an effort to grow our Fraternity both in numbers and in public
recognition, your Grand Lodge committeemen have led us in our participation
with the California State Teacher of the Year program and in bringing the
Masonic Student Assistance Program (MSAP) to local school districts across
our jurisdiction. These two programs are very effective in training and
recognizing our public school teachers and administrators and when one attends
either of these two types of events, the excitement among the participants is
electrifying.
The Ritual Committee consisting of the Grand Lecturer and five
Assistant Grand Lecturers, continued to bring a ritual competition program to
you. You responded in substantial numbers and it will be my privilege to
recognize the winners later during this Annual Communication.
In summary, the acts and activities of not only your Grand Master, but
of the Fraternity as a whole, are stunning both in quantity and quality.

48

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Now, let us turn to the general condition of Masonry in California. It
may perhaps best be measured by standing it up against the Strategic Plan of the
Grand Lodge of California. In that Plan, the strategic initiatives are
membership, lodge development, Masonic knowledge, leadership development,
Masonic center development and preservation, communications, community
involvement, and Masonic relief.
New applicants continue to knock at our doors because of local lodge
community involvement, the Internet, a seemingly constant flow of books and
movies (some fictional and some factual) produced by both Brethren of our
Craft and others. If we were able to remove the death factor from our statistics,
we would be reflecting a net gain in membership. While God is in control of
the death factor, we as Masons continue to labor for growth in our numbers. At
the close of this Masonic year, our records show that 1,910 applications were
received by our lodges, 1,737 Entered Apprentices were made, and 1,139
Brethren were raised to the sublime degree of Master Mason.
In the area of lodge development, we have not chartered any new
lodges this year, nor issued any dispensations that might lead to that end.
However, we have returned some charters that were under suspension when I
took the reins of office. Further, we have worked with Lodges that were
experiencing difficulties in membership activity and in filling leadership roles so
that they are in a more vibrant condition today due to their developing their own
plans of action under guidance from the Grand Master, the Executive
Committee, AGLs, Inspectors, appropriate committeemen of your Grand Lodge,
and their own members.
Masonic knowledge is making quantum leaps in both quantity and
quality in our jurisdiction due to the efforts of many of our committees.
Attendance at the Warden’s Leadership Retreats, Lodge Management
Certification Program modules, the Masonic Symposium, and Masonic
Formation offerings continues to rise at exciting rates. For example, attendance
by Lodge Wardens at the Warden’s Leadership Retreats grew by twenty percent
this year.
Through the efforts of many of our lodges and hall associations, in
tandem with the masonic properties committee and the Grand Lodge Executive
Committee, we are seeing many of our buildings being brought up to modern
standards, thus making positive impacts in the way that Masonry is viewed in
local neighborhoods and communities. One of the added benefits of these
efforts is the demand by Masonic and non-Masonic groups alike for rental
space, thereby generating cash flow for continued operations. In those areas
where excess properties are found due to consolidation of lodges, assistance is
being provided for the successful marketing and sale of them.
In any organization, communication is at the core of success. Masonry
in California is no exception. As a result, your leadership is encouraging the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

49

constituent lodges to be in constant contact with their members while, at the
same time, the Grand Lodge communications to local lodges and Masons
include the California Freemason Magazine, the Members Guide to Masonry in
California, and numerous educational materials, both written and electronic,
designed to assist in our personal education.
Masonic relief is not just a subject to be discussed, but one that calls
for individual, as well as, collective action. The flagship of our efforts in this
realm continues to be our Masonic Homes of California. The Union City and
Covina campuses are maintaining their service to our members in an
unparalleled manner. However, the activity which perhaps does not receive as
much publicity as do the campuses, but which is monumental in stature, is
Masonic Outreach Services; those services which, in fact, bring the relief
offered at the campuses directly into the private homes in which are membersin-need live. The Masonic Homes program, with all of its services, thrives
because of the charitable hearts of our Brethren.
Masonic relief is often measured in terms of dollars received in the
Grand Lodge Office of Philanthropy. This is not necessarily the most important
benchmark of caring, but seems to be one in which our Craft has much interest.
I can report to you that your philanthropic gifts to the Annual Fund amounted to
$1.47 million this Masonic year. The Annual Fund is designated for support of
the Masonic Homes of California and the California Masonic Foundation. This
is the highest level of total giving since 1987 and the first year since 1984 that
the total number of gifts has increased. I must report to you, however, that the
total number of donors is 7,097 out of a total membership of about 65,000, or
just eleven percent. My Brothers, we can do better.
Along side of these efforts at the Homes, is the local assistance
provided to Brothers, their families, and widows in our local communities.
These are services which receive little or no publicity, but which are just as rich
in caring and are provided by our local lodges.
I have been privileged this year to be a part of two new developments
which are moving from the drawing board to reality. I am referring to the
Acacia Creek residential housing projects situated on the campuses of our
Masonic Homes in Union City and Covina. Regarding these two equity housing
developments, the Acacia Creek Corporation Boards of Directors, the Board of
Trustees of the Masonic Homes of California, and this Grand Master established
a policy that these projects must each establish themselves as economically
viable with a threshold of 70% pre-sales of units before construction would
begin. These thresholds have been met, sparked by enthusiastic reception of the
projects by our Craft. Ground has been broken and the Cornerstone laid at
Union City. Ground will be broken in Covina early in the next Masonic year.
There are many other programs that affect the general condition of
Masonry in California as further noted here. The Deputy Grand Master and Sr.

50

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Grand Warden brought a concept to the Executive Committee as it relates to the
selection and training of Masonic District Inspectors. Their concept stems from
reports from Lodges, Assistant Grand Lecturers, and Inspectors, themselves,
that they need more training and that many are coming to the end of their
allotted time for service. Planning and execution of those plans are currently
underway to create an Inspector’s Academy to meet the demands of those so
intimately immersed in the tasks of an Inspector. While this Grand Master is
not personally involved in the development process, the Executive Committee
has received my whole-hearted approval to move ahead in order that the
Academy may come into existence early in the next Masonic year.
I am constantly amazed at the ingenuity demonstrated by you, my
Brethren. You continue to develop programs in your lodges; programs that are
designed to keep your membership interested and growing in membership
development and retention. You have concluded that Stated Meetings and
Degree nights alone are not getting the job done and you have developed social
and family oriented programs that are effectively working. My congratulations
to you; please keep up the good work.
Now, on a very personal note, may I ask express the gratitude that Judi
and I feel from your acceptance and broad support for our special projects this
year. You know that our hearts lie with the young people of our Masonic Youth
Orders and of our society in general.
At our Masonic Home in Covina, we have thirty six children under the
age of seventeen years. Our Project T-R-E-E (Toward Reunification,
Education, and Enrichment) has been supported by you to a level well beyond
our imagination. As a result of your funding of the Family Resource Center,
children and their parents are receiving education in right living and families are
being reunited and sent home as positive assets to their homes, neighborhoods,
and communities.
Judi is retired from her position as a public school children’s librarian,
but her passion continues to be the relationship between kids and books. Your
response to her requests for books and money to buy books has been
unbelievable. She has processed literally thousands of books and has received
funds to purchase thousands more, thus providing resources for the children
both within their residential homes on campus and in a central campus library
location. Parents and children are reading and growing together as a result of
your generosity.
Judi and I, the Covina children, and their parents thank you from the
bottoms of our hearts.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

51

Finally, relating to the condition of Masonry in this jurisdiction, I bring
to you the subject of “troubled Lodges and Masons”. For many years, the
Agenda for the required monthly meetings of your Executive Committee have
included an item entitled “Troubled Lodges”. At this point in my experience
and career, I am convinced that the label is not correct; that it should be
“Troubled Masons.”
I have found that there are virtually no Lodges that are in trouble as a
whole. Rather, there are Masons who are either not properly prepared to live a
Masonic principled life or who voluntarily choose to ignore it. Whether it be
their own fault or the fault of the Craft in general, we must take on the task of
educating, and in some instances re-educating, ourselves in the principles of
Freemasonry; those principles that are rooted in the obligations that we each
took at the Altar in our Lodges.
We must learn Masonic law as found in our California Masonic Code;
constantly grow in our knowledge of Masonic history and teachings; take
advantage of the ever-changing leadership and management educational
offerings of our Grand Lodge; indelibly imprint upon our minds those words
and actions found in our degree work; and always conduct our lives in a civil,
just, honest, truthful, and ethical manner. For if we do, my Brothers, we will be
respected by society and the profane and will be those men who demonstrate
that they can “best work and best agree”. Our Lodges will prosper and we will
be satisfied with ourselves.
As I have grown in my knowledge of Masonry this year, and I have
indeed done so, I have become convinced that certain additions or adjustments
should be made to our Code. Thus, I have submitted certain Grand Master
Decisions and Grand Master Recommendations for your consideration.
Grand Master’s Decision No. 1: Alternative Dispute Resolution. We
live in a litigious society, and sometimes that litigious nature invades the lodge
sanctuary of who best can work and best agree. Lodge and Grand Lodge
leadership can spend countless hours refereeing personal disputes between
brothers.
These disputes sometimes result in the filing of Masonic charges based
more on some caustic outburst than on any evidence of unmasonic conduct
under our Code. I believe that a Masonic Trial Master has the power to conduct
mediation between the antagonists before he renders his decision on whether a
Masonic trial should be conducted. With the assistance of legal counsel, a
protocol has been written for just such a mediation proceeding.
Mediation is appropriate when personal disputes between brothers
result in the filing of Masonic charges. However, mediation is not appropriate
when the Trial Master finds evidence in the charges which may warrant a

52

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

verdict of unmasonic conduct. In that case, the charges must proceed to the next
step in the trial process.
Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 1: Aligning the Inspector’s
Term to Coincide with the Grand Master’s Term. Our Code requires the Grand
Master to appoint the Inspectors who will serve with him each year. Currently,
however, the term of the Grand Master extends from Annual Communication to
Annual Communication, usually from about October 1 to September 30. The
Inspector’s term, on the other hand, runs according to the calendar year from
January 1 to December 31.
Thus, approximately one quarter of the Grand Master’s term may pass
before the new Inspector term begins. Aligning the Inspector’s term with that of
the Grand Master will help build a stronger and more united leadership team for
your Grand Lodge.
Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 2: Renaming Delinquent Dues
Committee to Member Retention Committee. As Grand Master, I have worked
with our Membership Development Committee to focus attention on member
retention. Other than membership losses due to death, Lodges lose members to
withdrawal and suspension for non-payment of dues.
Currently, the CMC states the duty of the Delinquent Dues Committee
as follows, “to personally, or otherwise, interview each delinquent member,
ascertain the cause, and work closely with the Master and officers in a
continuing effort to affect the retention of all deserving members unable to pay.”
The Membership Development Committee believes that the main
objective of a Lodge Delinquent Dues Committee, under its current definition, is
membership retention and that by renaming it so, a better understanding of its
purpose will result. Further, the new name will convey a more positive and less
punitive connotation. I agree with and support their findings and submit this
Recommendation to change the name of the Delinquent Dues Committee to the
Member Retention Committee.
Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 3: Defining the Duties of the
Fraternal Support Services and Lodge Support Committees. These two
committees were created under our Code in 2002. As time has passed, some of
their duties have become more or less necessary.
The Fraternal Support Services Committee created the role of the
Lodge Ambassador to assist in promoting programs that benefit the Lodge and
its members. This current Recommendation refines the size of the committee
and adds to the types of programs it will promote.
The Lodge Support Committee assists Lodges requesting help in the
areas of leadership development, financial planning, and community awareness.
This Recommendation refines the size of the committee and defines its
important role as a helpful resource to the Lodge.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

53

This Recommendation will define the committee duties in accordance
with the current practice as well as the needs anticipated for the future.
Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 4: Hazing. For the past few
years, several California Masons have witnessed, participated in, or experienced
candidate hazing. These actions, as reported, have included verbal intimidation
and abuse, bullying, and physical threats and activities. They have occurred
within the lodge room, within other building areas such as candidate preparation
rooms and restrooms, and outside of the building on the surrounding property
area.
More specifically, they have included the surrounding of a candidate
by groups of members who used threatening language as to what was going to
happen to him, physical rapping on the forehead, the requirement for kneeling in
such a manner that the candidate’s legs were kicked, the manhandling of a
candidate while he was blindfolded, and the use of very loud voices meant to
scare and intimidate on the part of certain participants in the Second Section of
the Third Degree.
Traditional methods of counseling by Lodge leadership, Lodge
officers, officer’s coaches, Inspectors, and AGLs have generally proven to be
unsuccessful. The most serious kinds of hazing are Masonic offenses already
and relate to serious bodily injury to a candidate. However, I believe that all
intentionally inflicted injury, whether serious or not, is against our Ritual, that
our law must clearly prohibit it, and that you should ask yourself if you would
be pleased to be hood-winked and then assaulted.
Hazing opens our Fraternity that so many of us refer to as “kind and
gentle”, to various forms of potential liability. It may be civil or criminal in
nature, but perhaps even more importantly, it may also be in the form of a total
loss of respect of us by both members and non-members.
Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 5: Grand and Glorious Order of
the Knights of the Creeping Serpent. The Grand and Glorious Order of the
Knights of the Creeping Serpent, also known as the Snakes, is an organization
consisting solely of Master Masons. It confers a degree using a ritual other than
the Ritual of this Jurisdiction. Under Section 1200.015 of the California
Masonic Code, this organization’s Constitution and Bylaws must be approved
by a majority vote of Grand Lodge at an Annual Communication before any
California Mason may be a member. Unfortunately, a number of California
Masons are already members, and Grand Lodge has never given the required
approval.
In September of 2007, this Grand Master, with the assistance of the
Grand Master of the Philippines, was able to meet and confer with the leaders of
the three Snake groups existing in California. The meeting was cordial and
friendly, conducted with Masonic harmony, and the leaders were informed of
their choices:
give up Snake membership, give up California lodge

54

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

membership, or seek to become legitimately recognized through legislation
under Section 1200.015 of the California Masonic Code.
The California leadership of this group did present a resolution for the
required approval before the March 31st deadline for legislation to be considered
at this Annual Communication. The resolution had the required three
proponents from three different lodges.
Unfortunately, through some
miscommunication, perhaps on my part, at least one of the proponents is not a
member of Grand Lodge. Therefore, I was required by Code to declare their
resolution out of order.
However, so that we are not required to wait another year before
considering their request, I have agreed to submit it for approval of their
Constitution and Bylaws as part of a Grand Master’s recommendation. I report
to you that their Constitution and Bylaws were submitted to Grand Lodge before
the opening of this Annual Communication.
If you adopt this recommendation, the Constitution and Bylaws of the
Grand and Glorious Order of the Knights of the Creeping Serpent, also known
as the Snakes, will be approved under Section 1200.015B of the California
Masonic Code, thereby permitting any Mason residing in this Jurisdiction to be
a member of such organization.
In closing, there are a number of “thank yous” that I must convey
because they are appropriate and reflective of my gratitude for the honor
bestowed upon me.
From the time that I was selected as the Jr. Grand Warden
Recommendee through the entire time that I served in the elected and
progressive line, I have been mentored by virtually all of the Past Grand
Masters. But, my time with Most Worshipfuls Vernon Gordon, Sam Pavlovich,
Ray Whitaker, Howard Kirkpatrick, David Doan, Fred Sorsabal, and Mel Stein
has been particularly rewarding as they spent one on one time teaching and
coaching me.
The elected Brethren of the Executive Committee serving with me this
year have been a part of a team that has been solid and committed to the
preservation and growth of Masonry in California. I refer to them as the
“Allstate Brothers” because under their continuing direction, Masonry is in
good hands. Thank you Larry, Ken, Bill, Frank, Allan, and Glenn.
The Grand Family this year has been just that; a family. The officers,
our ladies, and our teenagers have grown very close and will be friends forever.
Thank for serving your Grand Lodge this year.
A single Grand Master, or an Executive Committee, cannot manage
and govern this Grand Lodge of 65,000 Masons and 340 Lodges. It is
impossible. But with talented and dedicated Board presidents and members,
Committee chairs and members, and professional staff members in San

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

55

Francisco, Union City, and Covina, the work is accomplished. Thank you to
this wonderful team who have support me.
On a more personal note, my deep gratitude is extended to a long time
friend and Brother and his beautiful wife. Thank you, Cub and Joan Jack, for
filling the chair of Grand Marshal with all of the duties that are handled both in
and out of the Lodge room. Our friendship goes back to our days in high
school, DeMolay, Job’s Daughters, and Rainbow Girls and has never been
stronger than it is today. This year has allowed us to grow even closer together.
All of my love and thanks go to our remarkable family. Our children
and grandchildren have lived this year with us, and on many occasions without
us. You have been so understanding of our schedule and work load and
supported us on this entire journey to the East. Judi has done an outstanding job
as the First Lady of California Masonry and she has been the first and only lady
in my life for 44 wonderful years. Thank you; I love you.
When this 2008 Masonic year began, I developed an Emblem and a
Theme for the year. That Emblem is a grove of Giant Sequoia Redwood trees.
These trees are the largest living things on earth with many exceeding 300 feet
in height and being many centuries old. The most interesting fact regarding
them is that they do not stand alone; they cannot for they have no tap root.
Rather, they stand in groves in order that their shallow root systems may
intertwine with the result that they support each other.
The Theme this year has been Freemasonry Standing Strong. I find a
great parallel between a grove of giant Sequoias and our Masonic Fraternity.
When we as Brethren allow ourselves to stand along side of each other with our
roots established deeply in our Masonic principles and tenets, then we stand tall.
When we place our Craft lodges alongside of our Concordant and Appendant
Bodies, including our Youth Orders, then the Family of Freemasonry stands tall.
Brethren, upon concluding my year as your Grand Master and
reviewing the actions of you and your Lodges, I believe that we may truly say
that in California, Freemasonry is Standing Strong.
When I knelt at the altar of our Grand Lodge and obligated myself to
the office of Grand Master, I made the statement that I would walk with God
through this year. As I close my term as your elected leader, I trust that I have
done that. I have not been able to accomplish all that I had hoped for, but what
was completed, I believe, was done with my eyes looking up to the good of the
Fraternity because my head was bowed in reverence to God.
May God continue to bless this Grand Lodge of California so that it
may be a blessing to others.
Respectfully submitted,
Richard Wakefield Hopper, Grand Master

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

71

REPORT OF THE DEPUTY GRAND MASTER
2008/2009 Grand Lodge Budget
Larry L. Adamson, Deputy Grand Master
Our California Masonic Code Section 407.050 calls upon the Deputy
Grand Master to direct the preparation of the annual Grand Lodge budget based
upon the approved per capita set during the previous year by the Grand Lodge.
Attached you will find the proposed Grand Lodge Budget for the
period November 1, 2008 through October 31, 2009 which was carefully
prepared and reviewed and is recommended by your Grand Treasurer, Grand
Secretary, the Finance Department staff and me, as your Deputy Grand Master.
It has undergone extensive review by our Finance Committee which has
unanimously approved its recommendation.
This Grand Lodge budget reflects a 10.4% increase in overall revenues.
Based on a per capita of $31 and a more favorable membership of 61,520 (570
more members than projected last year), grants from the California Masonic
Foundation of $205,000 (an increase of $67,000), dividends from your Nob Hill
operations of $57,125, and higher investment distributions, total revenues will
exceed $2,580.000. Over 25% of the revenue used to fund your Grand Lodge in
2008-2009 is now being generated from non-per capita revenue sources.
The overall expenses are forecasted at $2,559,115, which reflect
funding for those strategic initiatives presented as part of the per capita proposal
last year and considers economic conditions that will likely be the reality of the
upcoming year.
Under the category of Fraternal expenses, funding has been increased
to cover greater costs associated with travel due to the anticipated impact of
high fuel costs, as well as premiums to provide for the Lodge Insurance
Program.
The Appropriations expense category accounts for 43% of the overall
increase in expenses for 2008-2009, but is reflective of the strategic initiatives
that are being implemented in this coming year and were part of the per capita
consideration approved this past year. Increased funding has been provided to
support continuing initiatives in Leadership Development, Masonic Education,
and Membership Development. This budget provides funding for the new
initiative to improve member communications by increasing the current
quarterly publication of the Freemason Magazine to a bi-monthly publication. It
also will provide funding for the roll-out of projects “Rebuild Our Youth
Orders,” and “Member Retention Support.” Additionally, funds are budgeted to
strengthen our Grand Lodge infrastructure in support of lodges through the
newly created Inspectors Academy, and will provide for dedicated Grand Lodge
staff support for Hall Association management and compliance.

72

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Increases in General and Administrative expenses reflect the two new
staff positions to support the member retention and Hall Association initiatives
as well as inflationary adjustments in employee salaries and medical/dental
benefits costs.
This is an exciting time to be a Mason in California, a time in which we
can solidify the future of our Craft for years to come. The budget before you is
reflective of the robust times we are now entering. Strong forecasted revenues
will allow us to aggressively pursue our strategic vision and provide greater
support to our members and our lodges. It is time for all of us to refocus
ourselves away from the mindset of organizational decline and to begin to lead
the efforts towards growth and expansion. This budget funds the beginning of
that vision and provides the infrastructure to support our emerging future.
Last year I said to you that I believe that California Freemasonry is
essential to the well-being of society today; and that we were poised to
experience of resurgence of interest in our craft. One year later, I now say we
are experiencing that resurgence and it is essential that your Grand Lodge
respond to that call. This proposed budget is focused squarely on that target and
I ask for your support.
Let’s all be a part of a generation of re-builders and guarantee that our
Masonic virtues are alive today and that we firmly set our sights on building for
a better tomorrow.
