81

INSTALLATION ADDRESS
LARRY L. ADAMSON
Grand Master of Masons in California, 2008-2009
Most Worshipful Sirs, my fellow Grand Lodge Officers, Brethren, Ladies and
Friends:
Seven years ago I was afforded the honor and privilege to stand at this
very lectern and address the brethren of this jurisdiction as it’s then Grand
Orator. It was a difficult time for me that day, not because I was unaccustomed
to addressing large groups, but instead, it was just 28 days after the tragic and
evil attacks of September 11th, 2001. That had a profound impact on what I had
planned to say! And, in that address, I told the brethren assembled that this
event would change the world forever. I told them that I believed it should
place a renewed importance on all Masons, whither so ever dispersed around the
globe to the relevance of the virtues and teachings of our Craft. I told them that
each of us must stand in direct and absolute defiance of the cowardly and
despicable acts of those who live by terror and hate. And that evil, no matter
how man attempts to disguise it, was still evil. Just seven years ago, I asked our
Lodge leaders and all California Masons to re-commit themselves to building a
stronger Craft in California so as to carry forward our teachings and principles
into our communities and to the world.
Today, seven years later, I stand before you as your 144th Grand Master
and profess to you that it is a “great time to be a Mason in California” and the
world! Whether inspired by my charge then, or their own sense of
responsibility, California Freemasonry has been and is building for a better and
stronger tomorrow.
So what is it that leads me to believe that our outlook is so very
optimistic? Let me share with you some information about the “State of the
Craft in California.”
After some five decades of member apathy, decline in membership,
and a general attitude of complacency, Freemasonry in California is changing.
For the last seven years, almost the entire decade of this century, we
have seen a substantial increase in the number of men seeking the light of
Freemasonry. In the past two years alone, from 2006 to 2008, our Lodges have
conferred in excess of 8500 Degrees. During this same period, we have seen
over 3000 men petition for membership in our constituent Lodges. In 2007
alone, our California Lodges made more Entered Apprentice Masons than in
any other year since 1988 and conferred more total degrees than any year since
the beginning of the 1990s. These trends clearly highlight a new attitude among
our members and our lodge leaders, and confirm what I believe and have

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

82

professed… that we are embarking upon a new era of exciting progress and
unparalleled opportunities here in California.
Seven years ago, the average age of our membership was just about 70
years. Today, the average has dropped to 64 years old. And the average age of
a new Master Mason is only 37 years old. The fastest growing segment of our
membership is 18 to 30 year old men. Our membership gains show that men,
young men, are being drawn to the principles and values of Freemasonry. So it
is time, right here and right now, today, to stop debating whether Freemasonry
is relevant in our society. You see, if we haven’t figured it out, society has.
Instead, I believe it’s time for us to ensure that we are prepared for the
emergence on our scene of this new generation and realize that our legacy will
be defined by whether we can stand up and deliver on our promise to provide
lessons, experiences, and mentoring, and apply the principles that show them
the path to becoming better men.
Meeting the expectations of our new members will be the true test of
our generation of Masons. So, I would ask each of you to stop for a moment
and challenge yourself as to:


What distinguishes you as a Mason?



How have you embraced the virtues and lessons of our craft?



How does your behavior and your daily actions set you apart?



Are you guided by brotherly love, relief and truth in your every
thought, act and deed?

You see, wearing a ring on one’s finger or a pin on one’s lapel, or even
wearing an apron, is not the true test of a Freemason. Instead, practicing out of
our lodges those duties which we have been taught in it, is the essence of
society’s interest in us and what I believe defines Applied Freemasonry.
Are we the ones to whom the burdened heart may pour out its sorrows?
The ones to whom the distressed may prefer its suit? And the ones whose
hands are guided by justice and whose hearts are expanded by benevolence?
Even more simply put, are we the ones who open the doors for others and aren’t
afraid to say hello to a stranger? The ones who will reach down and pick up
something dropped and in need of return to those who lost it? Are we the ones
who willingly give up our seats for someone who may need it more? And the
ones who, without prompting, call or visit the infirmed? Are we the ones who
will reach deep into our pockets to assist those less fortunate and in need?
Our newly received brethren are watching carefully and looking at us
to be examples of how we should live. “Applied Freemasonry” is the theme and
message I am bringing to you. This year, it emblematically is represented by the
rough ashler, or stone, denoting the imperfect state of human conscience. Yet,
from it, the smooth, or finished state, is evolving, which reminds us as Masons

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

83

that it is our obligation to labor zealously toward making ourselves better, and to
strive for the more perfect side of man. Added to this truly Masonic symbolism
is the phrase, “living the virtues today, building for a better tomorrow.” For I
believe that Freemasonry’s future will depend in large part on our commitment
to living every moment guided by virtue, and if we, by example, cause others to
simply “take note”, then Freemasonry will continue to be of interest to good
men all around the state.
The progress we have made in this decade has been a true partnership
of committed leadership, both from our Lodges and your Grand Lodge. And
I’m committed to seeing that this partnership of cooperation and strategic
thinking continues to be fostered and grown.
Five years ago, we adopted a philosophy aimed at institutionalizing
“strategic thinkers” throughout our Jurisdiction, based on a belief that merely
managing our institution, simply put, “wasn’t cutting it.” Our Grand Lodge
committed itself to a new “strategic thinking” model, and that model has been
and continues to be an essential part of the success that we are experiencing
today.
Two years ago, we undertook an effort to better align our Committees
to meet today’s emerging challenges. Our Lodge Support Committee was rechartered and now provides essential “Lodge Consulting Services” for those
Lodges that are facing challenges of leadership, membership and financial
difficulties. Manned by dedicated and professionally skilled members, this
committee has been an integral part in saving several Lodges from sure
consolidation and surrender. To its Chairman, Worshipful Miklos and Vice
Chair O’Brien, our craft in general, and those lodges in particular, are truly
indebted to your committee’s unselfish and tireless efforts to preserve
Freemasonry in these Lodges and most importantly in the communities they
serve.
We also re-chartered our Membership Committee and told its
leadership to take on a more broadly-defined membership process. One that
includes not only those who may have an interest in joining with us, but to
develop program support for the entire process of moving men from interest
through and including the successful ascension to the sublime degree of Master
Mason. Pass-it-on and On-the-level are but two of the soon to be “Three
Programs” aimed directly at this strategic challenge.
Strengthening Lodges must always be the single most important focus
of this Grand Lodge. Four months ago, with the support of Grand Master
Hopper and the Executive Committee, I asked the Grand Lecturer to join with
me in addressing what I characterized as a “system in need of change.” Over
the past several years of serving in a progressive leadership role, I observed a
vital support system that I believe was stretched beyond its ability to be
effective. Our five-division structure left our Assistant Grand Lecturers spread

84

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

unrealistically thin and spans of control were beyond any model used by
successful organizations. If we were going to be of aid and support to our
Lodges in their times of need, then this systemic problem had to be addressed.
We could not allow Lodges to suffer because our structure was inadequate to
provide them needed support. Effective today, we now have a nine-division
structure, and with it, a more realistic and manageable system of control. My
Brothers, our Lodges deserve no less of a commitment and I’m convinced that
our new team of Assistant Grand Lecturers will be our Grand Lodge’s “Dream
Team” and I thank each of them for their commitment to our future prosperity.
Much has been started in reshaping our direction for leadership
training. Two of our Membership Development Certification courses are now
high-tech and online, thereby broadening their availability to all lodges, and in
the next month the third module, Lodge Administration, will be available.
Three more modules are planned in 2009, bringing all of our training expertise
to you in the convenience of your home, on your time, and at your pace. I
would like to acknowledge the Chairman of Leadership Development,
Worshipful John Lowe, for his vision and support of this new and creative
thinking, and his committee’s commitment to being relevant to today’s needs.
Additionally, I would like to acknowledge our partner organization, American
Research Institution, for their expertise and commitment to create state-of-theart technology. Their Managing Partner is in our audience today, and I ask him
to please carry back to his staff our deepest appreciation for their support.
But that’s what has been accomplished. I would like to talk about what
is on the horizon. Those of you who attended our Annual Communication last
September heard me speak to the leaders of our Lodges about several priorities
that I feel are critical to our future. I asked for, and received, their support to set
forth a plan to rebuild our youth orders. My brothers, members of the Family of
Freemasonry in the most populated state in the country, we now have less than
5,000 members of our combined youth orders. These orders lack a Masonic
involvement. DeMolay chapters, Job’s Daughter’s Bethels and Rainbow
Assemblies continue to face closure due to a lack of adult leadership, and that is
criminal and unacceptable to this Grand Master, and I plan to change this.
Beginning today, I have restructured our Youth Orders Committee and given
them my own positive injunction. We will rebuild our orders and Masons will
lead that effort. We will begin by rebuilding our infrastructure of adult support
with a massive infusion of Masons available to participate in and lead advisory
councils, guardian councils and advisory boards. We will aid in seeing a
doubling of your participation in the next five years and at least a 25% increase
in Chapters, Bethels and Assemblies. For any young person who has a desire to
be affiliated, we will be available to answer that call. I’m serious about this
challenge and to prove it, I’ve asked two Past Grand Masters of this Jurisdiction
to help see that this is accomplished. Most Worshipful Sorsabal and Most
Worshipful Decker, thank you for stepping up as the new co-chairmen of these

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

85

efforts and for helping me to answer this essential call. But more importantly, I
challenge each of you to become involved and be a part of this vital effort.
Additionally, we will provide our Lodges with more sophisticated tools
to track and locate members and widows in our jurisdiction. Too many of our
members are being suspended simply because we loss track of their
whereabouts.
Suspending members who have had a thirty plus year
membership affiliation is not right, and this effort will help to eliminate these
losses. If we could eliminate these losses in our membership and continue our
new applicant growth, we would completely neutralize our losses due to deaths
and see a positive growth in California for the first time in decades.
The ownership and management of our Hall properties is a source of
much confusion and discord among our membership. Starting in October, a
full-time resource in the Grand Lodge office will be on staff and available to
assist our Masonic Properties Committee and, more importantly, our Hall
Associations in understanding proper administration and compliance associated
with the ownership of our precious Lodge properties.
Preparing for our future must be a continuing focus of this Grand
Lodge’s future. In order to better prepare the leadership of our Inspectorial
Corp, I am pleased to announce that the new and exciting Inspectors
Development Academy (the IDA) is nearing completion, and in February we
will begin our inaugural class. Under the leadership of Worshipful Vernon
Dandridge, this new academy will help us to attract the best and brightest in our
mid-management ranks and prepare them for success.
Each year, we in California are asked to support an effort sponsored by
the Grand Master, which is aimed at a charitable or community activity, known
as the “Grand Master’s Project.” This year is a little different; no, alot different
than a Grand Master’s project. It is a California Freemasonry project. I am
going to ask California Freemasonry to support me in a new, bold, and what I
believe is a grand concept and exciting venture for our Grand Jurisdiction. This
project, “A Partnership to Advance the Study and Understanding of
Freemasonry” places us at the forefront of Masonic research and education in
the realm of higher education.
You know, throughout our history, the Grand Lodge of California has
distinguished itself as a leader in the study of Freemasonry. Numerous Masonic
scholars have their roots here in California, and I have long felt that as leaders in
our fraternity we should be taking a much more expanded role in defining
Freemasonry to the world, rather than having the world define us.
Two weeks ago, your Grand Secretary and Director of Philanthropy
and I met with key officials at the University of California at Los Angeles and
reached a final agreement to form such a partnership. Capitalizing on our
relationship with the renowned academic scholar and foremost expert on

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

86

Masonic studies, and a member of the faculty at UCLA, this partnership will be
directed by Dr. Margaret C. Jacobs, a distinguished professor of history, along
with our Institute for Masonic Studies here at the Grand Lodge. Let me tell you
a little bit about Dr. Jacobs and what this partnership could mean to all of
Freemasonry.
She is, as I have stated, among the world’s foremost Masonic scholars.
Her work represents some of the most informative and comprehensive studies
on Freemasonry today. She has studied our historic role, particularly in areas
such as the rise of constitutionalism and civil societies. Her work has already
demonstrated how our Masonic values and tenets have influenced and guided
the development of constitutional democracies world wide.
Our partnership proposes to raise funds to establish and ultimately
endow a program titled “Faculty Research and Graduate Student Support at
UCLA.” This program will include:
1.

Faculty research in the area of Freemasonry and civil societies.

2.

Curriculum development for undergraduate courses on
“Freemasonry and its impact in America and other civil societies”
like that at the University of Oregon.

3.

Graduate student support for graduate scholars who are working
in, or have an interest in the history of Freemasonry.

4.

Support of an ongoing Masonic lecture series including worldwide
scholars in the research of our craft.

My brothers, let me be very clear. I don’t see this as a “Grand Master’s
Project.” I see this as an historic and bold venture on behalf of Freemasonry. I
believe that this partnership program will have far-reaching and very positive
benefits for all of us associated with the family of Freemasonry. My vision is to
see this become the model for the establishment of centers and facilities for
Masonic studies on additional university campuses both here in California and
throughout our country.
On Friday, Most Worshipful Onkst shared a very telling observation
with us here in this auditorium when he said that we no longer can be
represented by the Masonic phrase “To be one, ask one.” But today, instead, it is
to be one, you have to find one. Regaining a legitimate foothold with the
institutions of higher education will substantially expand society’s
understanding of our craft and our contributions to the advancement of civil
societies. I believe that it is this understanding that will help to bring men in our
doors. Working together with our Office of Philanthropy, I believe we can raise
the funding necessary to move this program towards becoming a true reality. I
look forward to working with our new Chairman of our Masonic Institute,
Worshipful Glen MacBride, and his board as we come together to complete this
important work.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

87

As optimistic as I am about our future and the interest of the younger
generation in our craft, I am also concerned about the behavior of some who
currently share the title of “Mason.” Too much time is being spent by your
Grand Lodge leadership in addressing issues of conduct and behavior, and that
must change. Men, and even organizations, within our fraternity that bring
discredit to our venerable institution have no place in California Freemasonry,
and this Grand Master is giving notice today that it is your time to either
reinvent yourselves or remove yourselves. There is no other option available to
you.
I would be terribly remiss if I didn’t take a moment to thank God for
the great blessings that he has brought to my life, that of my family. Many of
you know that I am a fifth generation Mason. It has always been my father’s
belief that Masonry would help him to become a better man. He has been and
continues to be a guiding light and I am truly blessed by his presence in my life.
Nine weeks from yesterday, I will be part of a first in California Freemasonry,
when I, as Grand Master, will have the honor and privilege to install my father
as the Master of Siminoff Daylight Lodge #850 in Union City. I think that is
going to be an emotional day! Ladies and gentlemen, please join me in
acknowledging my father, the Worshipful R. L. Doc Adamson.
I have also been equally blessed to have a partner in my life who has
never failed to support me in every venture I’ve chosen to pursue. She has and
displays the wisdom of Solomon, the patience of Job, and a herculean strength
of character. Lynn, I love you and I am so very excited that you will be with me
on this journey as California Freemasonry’s First Lady.
Brethren, ladies and friends, this is a great time to be a Mason in
California. I believe that our future has never been brighter, but I also believe
that we must move quickly to define our future. I hope that all of you, my
brethren, will answer this challenge and commit to applying our principles in
your every thought, act and deed. Applied Freemasonry means living our
virtues today and building for a better tomorrow. Together, let’s make it
happen!

91

REPORT OF THE SENIOR GRAND WARDEN
Per Capita Recommendation for 2009-2010
Kenneth G. Nagel, Senior Grand Warden

Each year your Senior Grand Warden is responsible for proposing the
future per capita assessment adequate to fund the obligations of our Grand
Lodge during his tenure as your Grand Master. Although this process has been
refined over the last decade, it must still begin a full four years before an actual
budget is finalized and is presented to the grand Lodge at our Annual
Communication. The present system of budget projections must include
forecasted future inflationary factors, resources to support strategic initiatives,
and most importantly, the 2009 membership projections. This comprehensive
and exhaustive budgetary process means that the budget upon which the per
capita is based is as accurate in forecasting our needs as is possible. In the past,
the financial staff has spent a substantial amount of time working with the
incoming Grand Officers, and their expertise has been invaluable. This year
was no exception.
After completing many months of work on this process, it is my intent
to present the Grand Lodge at our Annual Communication a forecasted revenue
and expense budget of $2,603,339. I further propose that a per capita to fund
the 2009/2010 budget be maintained at its current level, and request that you, as
members of Grand Lodge, support me in my decision not to raise per capita, but
instead retain the current rate of $31.00 per Master Mason on the roles as of
June 30, 2009.
The proposed budget, which is attached for your review, has been
thoroughly vetted by the Grand Lodge Finance Committee, Grand Treasurer,
Grand Secretary and Deputy Grand Master. Each have met either individually
or collectively with me to review the proposed budget for 2009/2010 and all
have agreed with me that this is a fair representation of our financial needs to
support our future obligations.
There are several factors that have a substantial effect on the budget
and the need for our revenue assessment known as “per capita.” The most
significant factor in the past four decades has been membership levels. As we
are all aware, a declining membership places disproportional pressure on the
amount of the per capita assessment. Although we are very encouraged in the
fact that for each of the last seven years, the number of new applications, the
number of conferred degrees and the number of raisings of new Master Masons
has increased from the preceding year, but we are still losing many of our senior
members as they complete their earthly toils. Taking into count all the
membership activity within our constituent lodges, the 2009/2010 analysis
projects a loss in membership of 2,320 members. While this is the lowest loss
of members in decades, this membership decline will result in lost revenue of

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

92

just under $72,000 or $1.21 on a per capita basis. Inflation, which is also a
constant concern, has been considered in this request and although is a projected
at a meager 1.5%, still equates to an increase of just over $30,000, or $.51 on a
per capita basis. Taken together, these two factors alone would dictate to us a
need to increase our per capita by at least $1.72 for the 2009/201 budget cycle.
But, we are also projecting many pluses, which I believe counter these
two negatives and which allows me to make a decision to keep the per capita at
its current rate. Some of them include.
1.

An increase in our endowment earnings and interest;

2.

Higher candidate fees to be collected due to your efforts in
increasing the number of Applications;

3.

A larger dividend from the Nob Hill Corporation due to
greater usage of our Grand Lodge Auditorium and facilities;
and

4.

A larger grant from the California Masonic Foundation due to
increased giving by our brethren to the Foundation.

The attached “Revenue Assumptions” exhibit will give you a fuller
explanation of how I have forecasted all our revenue categories for the
2009/2010 year.
On the expense side, your current Executive Committee has worked
collaboratively on many initiatives to ensure a seamless approach to our
programs and adherence to our Grand Lodge Strategic Plan as we transition
from Grand Master to Grand Master. My budget request includes your support
of a number of these continuing initiatives as well as three new strategic
initiatives, which I believe are essential to the immediate support of our lodges
and the future well-being of our craft.
We are seeing a true resurgence of interest in our Craft. Younger men
are knocking at our doors with a desire to join men of like-minded interests and
values to make a difference in their communities as well as their own lives. I
believe that our Craft is vital to our society, and California Freemasonry must be
ready to take on new challenges of the future. You and I as members of Grand
Lodge must remain focused if we are to meet those challenges that await us. I
think this budget request I am proposing will meet our needs. It is a “Grand and
Glorious day to be a Mason”, and your support will help to ensure that our
Masonic virtues remain alive and relevant for generations to come.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

93

REPORT OF THE GRAND TREASURER
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
At the 2007 Annual Communication, you approved a change in our
California Masonic Code allowing the Grand Treasurer to deliver to the Annual
Communication financial data for a partial year, through the prior July 31. That
change provides for your receiving information today that is relatively current,
rather than as of our prior fiscal year-end on October 31, 2007, some eleven
months ago.
The information I am going to give you today is for a nine-month
period from the start of this current fiscal year, November 1, 2007, through July
31, 2008. I will compare that information to like information for the same
period a year earlier. Further, this report is already posted on the Administrative
website under Forms/Annual Communication. It will continue to be available to
you there until the official proceedings of this Communication are published.
Those Proceedings will contain the customary audited financials for the entire
fiscal year.
It should be noted that this partial-year information has been prepared
internally by our fine financial staff and has not been audited.
As of July 31, 2008, your Grand Lodge had assets of $5,710,000 and
liabilities of $1,336,000 for Total Net Assets of $4,374,000. Those assets were
some 9.1% lower than at the same date a year earlier, largely due to the decline
in investment values. Keep in mind that most of the assets you might attach to
the California Grand Lodge are not actually owned by the Grand Lodge itself
and do not appear in these numbers. I am referring here to major assets such as
this facility, which is owned by the California Masonic Memorial Temple Corp.
and our homes in Union City and Covina, which are owned by the Masonic
Homes of California, both separate corporations.
The 12 months ending July 31, 2008 have been difficult ones in the
investment markets, due to the declines experienced since October 2007. The
investment accounts of the Grand Lodge are down by -7.2% during that 12month period, but have annualized gains of 5.7% per year for the prior three
year period, and 8.2% per year for the prior five-year period.
Your Grand Lodge continues to operate in the black on a year-to-year
basis. For the nine months ending July 31, 2008, the total income was
$1,727,500, while total expenses were $1,529,000. That income is some 1.2%
higher than the comparative period last year. Expenses for the nine-month
period ending July 31, 2008 were also higher than the same period last year, a
difference that is largely due to the timing of various expenses. While the Net
Excess as of July 31, 2008 is some $218,500, it should be noted that

98

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

approximately 20% of the total year’s expenses occur in the last quarter of the
year. The current projection is that the Grand Lodge will end the fiscal year on
October 31, with a slight surplus.
I continue to be most impressed with the concern and diligence which
your elected Executive Committee and the financial staff bring to the
monitoring of the actual income and expenses versus the budget. The numbers
are compiled and studied by all parties in great detail monthly, as the year
progresses. You should know that any surplus showing at the end of the fiscal
year is not carried over into the next year, but rather it is permanently
transferred to the Endowment Fund. The result is that each Grand Master starts
his year with a zero balance, with only the budget for that year to work with.
The nine-month expenses were divided in the following manner: 25%
for Fraternal matters (such as this Annual Communication, travel expenses of
the Elected and Appointed Grand Lodge Officers and Ritual Committee and the
insurance for the constituent lodges), 20% for Appropriations and Programs
(such as various lodge support Committees, the Freemason magazine, and
Committees such as Policy, Jurisprudence, Investment and Trial Review), and
52% to General and Administrative items (such as salaries and benefits,
accounting and auditing, investment management fees, legal fees and general
office operations). That breakdown compares closely to the same period last
year. General and Administrative Expenses are some higher than a year ago,
largely due to the use of temporary employees to fill vacancies in the permanent
staff.
I again pay tribute to the many highly dedicated brothers who serve on
the various Boards and Committees of this Grand Lodge and its related entities
for the extraordinary amount of time, energy, dedication and hard work they
devote to the business and fraternal affairs of our California Masonic
community. Each of you should seek them out and give them a hearty “Thank
You” for the work they do.
As you know, our Past Grand Secretary, Very Worshipful John
Cooper, retired this past April, after serving this Grand Lodge for some 16
years. It was my honor and pleasure to work closely with Brother John for the
last year and a half of his service. During that time I developed an extreme
admiration for the experience, fraternal feeling, steady leadership and devotion
he brought to that position. I am extremely grateful for the opportunity to have
benefited from his example in that close relationship.
During the past two years, I have had the opportunity to have that same
close relationship with our now Grand Secretary, Very Worshipful Allan
Casalou. I have watched the long and careful planning which went into his
taking the job and the seamless transition that has resulted. Allan has built
around him a well organized team of talented and dedicated people, some who
have been here for years, and some who are new. I marvel at the well

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

99

orchestrated and smooth operation of the many functions which our Grand
Lodge office performs under his leadership and example. I can tell you, my
Brethren, we are extremely fortunate to have such a talented Mason and leader
to fill this all-important position. I look forward to the continuation of our
working relationship.
And finally, my brethren, I thank each of you for having allowed me to
work with you and for you during this past year. I am extremely proud and
honored to be a part of the California Masonic Grand Lodge team.
Respectfully submitted,
Glenn D. Woody, Grand Treasurer

APPENDIX E

103

REPORT OF THE GRAND SECRETARY
Most Worshipful Grand Master and members of the Grand Lodge,
I am pleased to present this, my first report, to you as your Grand
Secretary. As you know, I assumed this office on April 1, 2008, at the
retirement of our 10th Grand Secretary, the Very Worshipful John L. Cooper III.
John served our Grand Lodge for over 17 years. During that time, he led many
significant accomplishments that have resulted in the strong foundation on
which I stand today. But even more than that, John has been a good mentor, a
fine example, and a true friend to me and to many of us. While we know his
contributions to Freemasonry will certainly continue, please join me now in
extending to Very Worshipful Brother John a warm round of applause in
recognition of his many years of dedicated service.

Lodge and Membership Information
As of today there are 343 chartered lodges in our jurisdiction, including
four research lodges. We began the fiscal year with 348 lodges and five were
consolidated. There is one Masonic club, Prometheus Club, which formed this
year for the purpose of establishing a Masonic Lodge. This club has requested a
Dispensation, but because of the existing laws regarding such a request, their
request cannot be acted upon until after the Annual Communication.
Our membership at the beginning of the fiscal year was 65,711. We
had 1,985 gains and 4,199 losses resulting in an ending membership on June 30,
2008 of 63,497. While we still recorded a loss of 2,214 members, this is the
smallest loss of members in this jurisdiction since 1968. A more detailed report
of membership activity is included in my written report to be distributed later.

Corporate Services
The Grand Secretary serves as the corporate secretary to the boards that
work together with the Grand Lodge to carry out our overall mission. These
boards include the Acacia Creek Communities, the California Masonic
Foundation, the California Masonic Memorial Temple, the Masonic Homes of
California, and the Nob Hill Masonic Center, Inc. The Grand Secretary’s office
provides centralized administration, finance and accounting, human resources,
fund raising, and information technology services to these entities. This year we
added centralized communications, board governance and legal counsel.
Today a cabinet of talented and dedicated professionals assist the
Grand Secretary in carrying out these responsibilities and delivering first rate

104

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

business services to the Grand Lodge and its related entities. I wish to express
my gratitude to this incredible team:
Tom Boyer, Chief Financial Officer
Brad Colby, Board Governance Manager
Doug Ismail, Chief Philanthropy Officer
Terry Mendez, Director of Communications
Linda Schomaker, Chief Human Resources Officer
John Wickey, Chief Technology Officer

Philanthrophy
Our Code directs the Grand Secretary to oversee the collection of
contributions to the Masonic Homes and our other charitable organizations. To
that end, the Grand Secretary maintains an Office of Philanthropy, led by
Brother Doug Ismail, Chief Philanthropy Officer. I am pleased to report that
more than five million dollars was received in donations this year. Nearly $1.5
million was received through the annual fund drive alone, which is the highest
amount raised through the annual fund in any year since 1987. You can read
more detail about our fund raising efforts and charitable giving programs in the
2008 Annual Report to Members, published for the first time this year and
distributed at this Annual Communication.

Program Services
The Grand Secretary’s office provides support to the many programs of
the Grand Lodge in the area of communications, membership development,
leadership development, and Masonic education and formation.
In the area of communications, this year we launched a new member
and public Web site and published our first electronic newsletter, called The
Leader. We are proud that our new Web site at www.freemason.org was
selected by the Society of National Association Publications as the second best
Web site among more than 750 membership organizations in the United States;
awarding our site silver awards in general excellence and design. The Leader
will be distributed electronically to all Lodge and Grand Lodge leadership on a
monthly basis providing timely information on valuable programs and services.
Over 1,200 people participated in our leadership training programs this
year, which included two Inspectors Seminars, four Wardens Leadership
Retreats, two Secretaries Retreats, five Lodge Management Certification
Programs, and five Masonic Formation Certification Programs. In addition, two
training programs are offered online providing valuable instruction on Lodge
Finance and Hall Association Management.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

105

In the area of membership development, this year we published the first
Member’s Guide to Masonry, providing all of our members with useful
information about their membership in our craft and how to access the many
resources available to Masons and their families in this state. We also published
a member retention guide for Lodges to use in their efforts to effectively retain
their membership.

Recognition of Foreign Grand Lodges
It is my responsibility as the Grand Secretary to keep the Grand Master
informed on matters pertaining to the recognition of foreign Grand Lodges and
to present to you in the form of a motion the recognition of certain Grand
Lodges which meet our criteria therefore. Recognition is the granting of
authority for Masons in this jurisdiction to have Masonic Communication,
which is communication involving the esoteric or secret portion of the Ritual,
with Masons of a Lodge chartered by or holding allegiance to the recognized
Grand Lodge. In practice, this generally means Masons who belong to Lodges
chartered by recognized Grand Lodges may visit each other’s tiled meetings.
The terms of recognition may also include the privilege of dual membership.
Our Grand Lodge may recognize only Grand Lodges that:
A.

Are regularly formed by subordinate Lodges which trace their
origin to regular and legitimate Ancient Craft Masonry;

B.

Hold undisputed sway as the acknowledged sole or concurrent
supreme power in Ancient Craft Masonry in the territory in which
they claim jurisdiction;

C.

Confine their authority and the exercise thereof to the three
degrees of Craft or Symbolic Masonry; and

D.

Subscribe to the General Regulations of Masonry.

We are aided in the evaluation of Grand Lodges seeking recognition by
the Commission on Information for Recognition, a committee of the Conference
of Grand Masters of Masons of North America. We are honored in California
that our very own Past Grand Master, Most Worshipful M. William Holsinger,
is a member of that important Commission.
Three Grand Lodges, which have requested mutual recognition with
our Grand Lodge, have been found by the Commission to meet the criteria for
recognition.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

106

The Grand Lodge of Bosnia and Herzegovina
The Grand Lodge of Bosnia and Herzegovina was consecrated
on April 16, 2005 by the Grand Lodge of Austria with the
support of the Regular Grand Lodge of Slovenia and is
comprised of Lodges recently chartered by those Grand
Lodges.

The Grand Lodge of Mauritius
The Grand Lodge of Mauritius was consecrated on March 12,
2005 by the French National Grand Lodge and is comprised
of eleven lodges; eight previously constituted by the GLNF
between 1992 and 2004, one Scottish lodge dating back to
1864, one English lodge dating back to 1877 and one French
lodge dating back to 1778.

The Grand Lodge of Macedonia
The Grand Lodge of Macedonia was consecrated on
September 30, 2005 by the United Grand Lodge of England
and is comprised of lodges recently chartered by that Grand
Lodge.

Most Worshipful, I move that the Grand Lodge of California grant
mutual recognition to: the Grand Lodge of Bosnia Herzegovina, the Grand
Lodge of Mauritius, and the Grand Lodge of Macedonia.

Motion ADOPTED.

Prince Hall Grand Lodges
It has been the practice of this Grand Lodge to extend recognition to
Prince Hall Grand Lodges which meet our criteria for recognition and where
concurrent jurisdiction has been established by the Grand Lodge we also
recognize in the same jurisdiction. To date, we have extended recognition to 27
Prince Hall Grand Lodges in the United States with lodges chartered in 33
states, the District of Columbia, Guam and Turkey.
As Grand Secretary, I try to stay apprised of the recognition activity of
our sister Grand Lodges across the country and find that there are four
additional Prince Hall Grand Lodges that meet the above criteria for

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

107

recognition. Our recognition will be on the same basis that recognition was
previously exchanged with the Grand Lodge sharing the same jurisdiction.

The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge
Jurisdiction of Oklahoma
The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge Jurisdiction of
Oklahoma was consecrated in 1893. In 1994, the Grand
Lodge of F.& A.M. of Oklahoma entered into a compact of
recognition with this Prince Hall Grand Lodge.

The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Nevada
and Jurisdiction, Inc.
The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Nevada and
Jurisdiction, Inc. was consecrated in 1960. The Grand Lodge
of F.& A.M. of Nevada entered into a compact of recognition
with this Prince Hall Grand Lodge on November 8, 1999.

The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge Free and
Accepted Masons of North Carolina and Jurisdictions,
Inc.
The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge Free and
Accepted Masons of North Carolina and Jurisdictions, Inc.
was consecrated on March 1, 1870. The Grand Lodge A.F.&
A.M. of North Carolina entered into a compact of recognition
with this Prince Hall Grand Lodge on September 20, 2008.

The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge
Jurisdiction of Texas
The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge Jurisdiction of
Texas was consecrated in 1875. The Grand Lodge A.F.&
A.M. of Texas entered into a compact of recognition with this
Prince Hall Grand Lodge on April 23, 2007.

Most Worshipful, I move that the Grand Lodge of California extend an
offer of mutual recognition to: the Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge
Jurisdiction of Oklahoma, the Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge of
Nevada and Jurisdiction, Inc., the Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge
Free and Accepted Masons of North Carolina and Jurisdictions, Inc., and the
Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge Jurisdiction of Texas.

Motion ADOPTED.
Appreciation
As I conclude my report, I wish to extend my appreciation and
gratitude to the 343 lodge Secretaries and 155 Assistant Secretaries in this
jurisdiction whose dedicated work is the backbone of our lodges and our Grand
Lodge.
I would also like to thank the Assistant Grand Lecturers and District
Inspectors who work so diligently with the Grand Secretary’s office on
numerous administrative matters throughout the year.
I have been given the great fortune in my first year as Grand Secretary
to work with Very Worshipful Glenn Woody as our Grand Treasurer. Glenn
has been an extraordinary colleague and counselor to me this year and to him, I
owe a great debt of gratitude.
I am blessed to work with an incredible staff of men and women at the
Grand Lodge Office, including our offices in Covina and Union City, whose
service to our Grand Lodge is fueled by the good work that each of you do
every day in your communities throughout the state.
It has been my privilege over the years, and this year especially, to visit
many of our lodges and I am continually impressed and inspired by your
dedication and enthusiasm for our fraternity. You make this job important and
special to me and it is my honor to serve you.
Finally, I wish to thank Most Worshipful Richard Wakefield Hopper,
who has led our Grand Lodge this year with dignity and distinction. While I
hope to work as Grand Secretary for a number of Grand Masters in the years to
come, you, Most Worshipful, will always be the first among those Grand
Masters and I thank you so much for the confidence you have expressed in me,
for your unconditional assistance and guidance, and for your precious
friendship. Thank you.
Respectfully submitted,
Allan L. Casalou, Grand Secretary

APPENDIX F

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

127

REPORT OF THE GRAND LECTURER
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
California Masonic Code section 404.250 directs me to make a report
to you at each Annual Communication, outlining our activities during the year
and the condition of the Craft within this Jurisdiction. Before doing so,
however, I must again thank you for electing me to this office, thus giving me
the opportunity to travel throughout the State, meeting you, and enjoying with
you the many ways you celebrate Masonry. More importantly, I've been
permitted to continue to try to walk in the image of those who preceded me in
this position.
Much this year has been very encouraging. I believe the quality of
Masonic education continues to improve. We still stress the role of the Officers'
Coaches in teaching out ritual, and they have clearly blossomed in the
assumption of this responsibility. We hear of more and more candidates who opt
to do full form proficiencies, with strong support and encouragement from their
lodges, thereby ensuring a firmer foundation for the spiritual buildings they
hope to erect. As is our custom, we've held two ritual conferences at which the
Inspectors demonstrated their proficiency in all three degrees. The AGLs have
conducted Master Mason Seminars in each Division, some of which I've been
able to attend and assist. Of course, the Inspectors each conduct the everpopular District schools of instruction, at which officer attendance is mandatory.
I continue to be impressed by the dedication shown by all who have so willingly
assumed the responsibility for teaching our ritual.
This year saw another Ritual Competition, limited to individual lodges,
and requiring participation by the four principal officers. We are pleased that all
five Divisions were represented, with 18 lodges competing. Each was judged in
minute detail in their performance of the First Degree. The results were
exemplary. All should be applauded for their obvious hard work and diligence.
We will present awards to all finalists and the winner at the banquet on
Saturday.
We, though, are not without concerns. Too many lodges have difficulty
in finding men who are willing to serve as officers. The Lodge Support
Committee, and others, spend too much time trying to defer consolidations and
defuse intra-lodge disharmonies. Somehow we have forgotten our goal of seeing
who best and can work and best agree in an arena where no contention should
ever exist. We have identified and successfully prosecuted instances of
candidate hazing. As you will learn, our efforts will continue until those others
who might be inclined to indulge in these vicious practices will have gotten the
message, and it, or they, are totally eliminated from our ceremonies.

128

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

We have embarked upon two new programs which are very exciting
and predict great promise. First, is the Inspector Development Academy, which
you will come to know as the IDA. This program, scheduled to begin early next
year, will identify a select group of brothers who have demonstrated their ability
to be potential Inspectors. Once accepted, each will be enrolled in an intensive
course of instruction preparatory to their possible appointment to that position.
Our goal, obviously, is to create a pool from which we may select members who
are ritualistically proficient and have developed the various skills requisite to a
confident and qualified Inspector.
Secondly, it was apparent that trying to oversee and assist the entire
Jurisdiction with only five AGLs has become increasingly unrealistic. A careful
analysis of the State determined that it would require more AGLs to provide
complete and conscientious service to our lodges and their members. Hence, a
most of you know, our next Masonic year will begin with nine Assistant Grand
Lecturers. Neither the number of Districts nor Inspectors has been reduced. Our
intent is to relieve the demands for travel time and make an AGL more
immediate and available to every Division and District. To that end, I have
asked every AGL to personally visit at least one District school for each District
in his Division each year.
While we are adding a number of new AGLs, we are losing two current
ones, Glenn MacBride and Dale Armstrong. Both have given unstintingly of
themselves. Their dedicated and conscientious work on our behalf has been
invaluable to me. They both shall be sorely missed. I’m pleased, though, that
both have accepted important committee assignments.
I’ve often said our only product is our ritual. Those ceremonies
embody all the promises we make to every candidate. I cannot close this year
without again expressing my profound thanks to my friends and colleagues, the
Inspectors and AGLs for their untold efforts in protecting and perpetuating our
ritual. Let us not forget, however, WE are only the conduit. It is YOU, my
Brethren, who shoulder the responsibility for the meaningful communication of
our lessons. It is YOU who realize the rewards of successfully guiding a
candidate through our degrees. It is YOU who get to enjoy the satisfaction of
the knowledge of a job well done when you watch a new Master Mason grow
into Masonic maturity as a result of your labors.
Finally, Most Worshipful, June and I have had a wonderful time as part
of your Family, and we thank you and Judi for the pleasure of serving with you.
Thanks, also, to John Cooper and Allan Casalou and the entire Grand Lodge
staff for their untiring, unwavering aid and assistance. But, to all of you, my
Brethren, I offer my immense gratitude for your dedication and devotion, and
for all you do to keep our Fraternity alive and thriving. I’m honored by the
warmth and hospitality you’ve shared with us whenever we were fortunate
enough to be with you.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

Thank you again for allowing me to serve as your Grand Lecturer.
Respectfully submitted,
Paul D. Hennig, Grand Lecturer

APPENDIX G

133

GRAND CHAPLAIN’S MESSAGE
by The Very Reverend Arthur H. Weiss
Thank you, Most Worshipful, for this opportunity to address our
Family of Freemasonry this morning, and for the honor of serving you and this
Grand Lodge. Barbara and I will be forever grateful to you and Judi.
Standing in the dank dungeon cell, surrounded by his fellow prisoners,
he awaited the inevitable interrogation and torture by the Inquisition. Knowing
they would all suffer a similar fate, he struggled to find a way to bring a shred of
hope, a moment of inspiration, to the weary band of fellow countryman.
Straightening up to his full height, he began to speak. . . . .
And thus began one of the great stories in European history. But the
rest of this story did not take place in France in 1307, but 300 years later in
Spain, in the pages of a fictional novel, first published in two parts, in 1605 and
1615. Don Quixote de La Mancha, by Miguel de Cervantes, was an instant best
seller, became one of the world’s greatest fictional works, and was ultimately
voted the “Greatest Book of All Time” by the Nobel Institute.
Over the last 400 years the story of Don Quixote has been analyzed and
interpreted in much depth, and always within the perspective of the norms of
current society. Since the time of its first publication, countless books, plays,
musicals, movies and even television have explored its themes, its nuances, and
borrowed from or copied its characters. But one underlying constant has been
Cervantes’ expression of simple chivalric morality and its constant battle with
an increasingly complex world. A similar observation can be made of
Freemasonry, as our philosophy and basic tenets of brotherly love, relief and
truth continually battle the forces that would destabilize and erode society.
Perhaps the best interpretation in our modern times of that chivalric
ideal and its meaning in our lives came in 1965, when composer Mitch Leigh
and lyricist Joe Darion penned a score for the Broadway production of Man of
La Mancha, an adaptation of the Cervantes story set within a second tale of an
imprisoned actor. In that score débuted a song which captured not only the
hearts of musical theater-goers, but was instantly adopted as a statement of
hope, as a personal theme, or as an ode to the future by numerous graduating
classes.
The Impossible Dream is a quintessential expression of the human
spirit searching for the unreachable light of pure truth. It outlines the moral and
behavioral characteristics which are essential to that purity of life and conduct
so necessary to gaining admission into the celestial lodge above. As such it is a
defacto anthem for Freemasonry, as you only need to repeat the lyrics to be
reminded of our tenants.

134

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

To dream the impossible dream reminds us to set big goals as we never
stop trying to improve ourselves. To fight the unbeatable foe urges us to believe
in ourselves and our convictions and remain standing strong in the face of
opposition. To bear with unbearable sorrow teaches that no matter how cruel
life may seem to be, we must rise and start again and not be deterred from our
ultimate goal. To run where the brave dare not go inspires us to face every
challenge, every obstacle with courage and fortitude.
To right the unrightable wrong demands that we stand for justice with
an eye toward charity, that we be ever willing to extend a helping hand to the
downtrodden and oppressed. To love pure and chaste from afar, that we look
for the nobility in every person and practice the tenant of brotherly love without
question. To try when your arms are too weary, to reach the unreachable star
that we must never stop seeking the pure light of truth, that divine essence in
each of us, the understanding of our inner soul.
Dedicating ourselves, then, to the pursuit of these tenants ultimately
brings the self expression of our commitment, our reason for being as Masons,
our vow to persevere no matter how difficult life may be.
This is my quest, to follow that star,
No matter how hopeless, no matter how far
To fight for the right without question or pause
To be willing to march into hell for a heavenly cause
But what is the result of following this quest? It is the understanding
of our Father’s plan for us, His eventual reward for remaining faithful to the
search for knowledge and truth.
And I know if I’ll only be true
To this glorious quest,
That my heart will lie peaceful and calm
When I’m laid to my rest

And of what benefit to our society and our fraternity is this
commitment, this vow of chivalry? And the world will be better for this imagine if all God’s children were dedicated to such a purpose, peace and
harmony would indeed pervade the world! That one man scorned and covered
with scars, still strove with his last ounce of courage means that it starts with
just one of us, no matter our station in life. We must each choose to lead,
setting the example, walking the talk, standing strong in Freemasonry. Then,
and only then, can each one of us, each human being, be free to pursue their
dreams, to pursue the light of knowledge and truth, to prepare ourselves – to
reach the unreachable star!

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

135

Brethren and ladies, dream impossible dreams. Be true to this glorious
quest of Freemasonry. And never stop reaching for the unreachable star!
GRAND LODGE OPENING
FRIDAY AFTERNOON SESSION
SEPTEMBER 26, 2008
Great Architect and Ruler of the Universe,
We thank you for allowing us to gather this day, in this beautiful place,
to perform our duty of conducting the business of this Grand Lodge at its 159th
Annual Communication.
We ask that you bless the members of this Grand Lodge. Endue them
with the fortitude to stand strong for this jurisdiction in the work before us, to
listen with openness, to judge with wisdom, and to embrace our vision for the
future, unshackled by personal agendas, maintenance of the status quo, or
unwillingness to take the path less traveled. Help us to make what’s right, our
might, and keep the principles of this great Fraternity standing strong.
Bless these Grand Officers as they complete their service to this Grand
Lodge. Returning to our ranks, help them make a difference by sharing the light
of their experiences in their communities.
And above all, dear Lord, bless this Grand Master. Let him lead our
deliberations with the wisdom, grace and inner strength he has demonstrated
throughout this year. Keep he and Judi safe on the road ahead and always
standing strong in Freemasonry.
We ask these things, oh Lord, that we may better live to serve thee, and
in your name we pray.
Amen.

APPENDIX H

141

50 YEAR DEDICATION
CALIFORNIA MASONIC MEMORIAL TEMPLE
by Mark N. Gibson, Grand Orator

In reviewing the original planning and construction of the building of
this Memorial Temple as given in the 1958 Annual Proceedings, a brief
historical review of the principal Masonic Temples associated with the growth
of this Grand Lodge over the last 158 years may be appropriate.
The three story “Red House” on the corner of 5th and J streets in
Sacramento is where our Grand Lodge was originally formed, four months
before California became a state. While we held no financial interest in the
building, we likely could have but the 103 Masons from three lodges at the
original formation chose rather to build the temple of humanity in the hearts of
men, for rather than invest in a building, the record shows that they donated
over $33 thousand dollars of personal funds in a single year towards the relief of
the needy as a result of the several cholera epidemics of the time.
The first Masonic Temple partially owned by the Grand Lodge was
erected in San Francisco at the height of the Civil War. Located at Post and
Montgomery Streets, the cost was $250 thousand dollars. It was started in 1860
and completed in 1870. That Temple served the Masons of California for over
40 years.
Further research of the records shows that in 1904 Masonry in
California was experiencing rapid growth and felt the need for more expansive
quarters. In April 1906, the great earthquake and fire expedited that search and
in 1908 a site was purchased at 25 Van Ness. The cornerstone was laid in
October 1911 and dedicated at the Annual Communication in 1913.
In 1947 Freemasonry in California was again experiencing rapid
growth, and a committee was appointed to research a new location and Temple
and the Committees worked for 11 years prior to the opening of this Memorial.
In 1955 our Grand Master Henry C. Clausen stated “There are parts of
this world where Masonry has been blotted out-where the winds of freedom
are stilled-where no man dare show a Masonic emblem. A Mason in such a
place would consider it a heaven sent blessing to be permitted to contribute to
a mighty Masonic Memorial for all the world to see. It is truly an American
Masons privilege”. He appointed Past Grand Master and Grand Treasurer
Edward H. Siems to chair the Committee on the California Masonic Memorial
Temple.
Prior to the groundbreaking, over 70 thousand masons had made
donations towards this monument amounting to over 1 million 223 thousand
dollars towards the full costs of 5 ½ million dollars. Stock was sold of our

142

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

interest in the Temple on Van Ness and a call to the Masons of California went
out. Lodges were asked to donate the equivalent of one days wages for each
member towards the construction, and have their names recorded in the “Open
Book”, which remains on display in our lobby.
Six San Francisco banks agreed to loan the California Masonic
Memorial Temple, a California Corporation, 50% of the cost of construction and
land, not to exceed 3 million dollars, to be paid within 10 years at 4% interest.
Several strikes in 1956 delayed construction and the anticipated
completion in 1957, but the building was finally complete and the cornerstone
laid at the annual communication in 1958, with all the pomp and grandeur of
our Grand Jurisdiction.
It remains a landmark on Nob Hill. It is a convention center, an opera
house. a concert hall, a playhouse, a theatre, an exhibit space, a library, a
museum, a patriotic center, a community auditorium and a home to over 60
thousand Freemasons in California. More importantly it remains a monument
dedicated to our brother Masons who have made the ultimate sacrifice in
support of the freedoms we enjoy and the principles of theses United States.
We are grateful of the prudent management and dedication of the
California Masonic Memorial Temple Board of Directors, in its various
manifestations through the years, for the wise and prudent management of this
magnificent edifice, which we know will continue to be a landmark in San
Francisco and for Freemasonry for many generations to come.
I would like to close with the paraphrased remarks of Edward H.
Siems, Past Grand Master at its dedication in 1958. Of the so many involved in
its construction he is the most likely Father of this Memorial Temple.
“Most Worshipful Grand Master, on behalf of all the Masons in
California, it’s 339 Lodges and the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge of
California, who being animated with a desire to promote the honor and
interest of the Craft, have erected and furnished this Temple their
convenience and accommodation.
Erected as a memorial honoring their heroic dead , this Temple is to
be dedicated to Freemasonry and universal benevolence, that the cause of
freedom for which they died shall ever be therein the sacred trust of their
brethren and of Freemasons yet unborn. If this building meets with your
approval, they are desirous that it be dedicated according to the ancient form
and usage.”

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

143

GRAND ORATION
Most Worshipful Grand Master, Past Grand Masters, Distinguished and
Honored Guests, members of the Grand Lodge and Brothers all.
I have been honored to serve as your Grand Orator for this past year.
Honored to serve with an outstanding corps of Grand Lodge officers and their
ladies who have exemplified the Tenets of Freemasonry in their every action,
representing you well at the various Cornerstones and events this past year.
Our Grand Master Richard Wakefield Hopper, and the first lady of
Freemasonry, Judi, have exemplified the principles of our Fraternity in word
and in action. It has been my distinct privilege to serve with such dedicated,
intelligent and committed leaders of our Craft and on behalf of all the Grand
Lodge officers, thank you, Most Worshipful, for a once in a lifetime experience
that we will never forget.
I was honored to speak at 19 Cornerstones this year and I endeavored
to express California Freemasonry’s commitment to a free public education, our
appreciation for the hard work of our dedicated public school teachers and to
share a bit about the history and principles of our Craft. I learned early on that
the attention span of 50 third graders is somewhat different than the members of
my Lodge, but probably not all that different from this Grand Lodge at this
hour, so I promise to be brief.
A couple of years ago, I had a conversation with my Brother-In-Law
and my best friend. I informed them about a local restaurant that had been
closed and was in foreclosure. They had both been very successful in business
and real estate and we bandied about ideas of what might be a good business for
the location.
One idea that garnered the most enthusiasm was for a clubhouse. I
suggested that it already had a kitchen, we could set up a big screen TV and
surround system, watch movies with the kids or share in the big Game on
Sundays, maybe put in a pool table and a card room. Because we wanted our
families involved, it was suggested that, while we might invite friends to join us
on occasion, only those we completely trusted, who were upstanding in the
community and with their families could join the club, and then only when all
the other members agreed.
They were enthusiastic, to the point of being willing to invest
thousands of dollars to pursue this exciting enterprise.
Then I pointed out that we already had such a place, our Lodge, of
which we were all members. It’s just that we weren’t running it that way.
Somewhere along the way we lost the vision of what it could be.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

144

What happened?
The large influx of members in the late 40’s and early 50’s required
that we emphasize ritual, and insure its accuracy. Because of the demands of
initiation and precision, many of the traditional programs and activities of the
Lodge became secondary as we endeavored to accommodate all who asked.
Along the way we seemed to loose fellowship and replaced it with
ritual.
To this day the one codified requirement that we require of our senior
Lodge leaders is proficiency in the ritual. There is no requirement of fellowship,
or of our officers having any management or leadership experience. We have
limited the definition of “Masonic Labor” of that of making Masons.
How many brethren do you know that joined the Craft because of a
love of ritual?
That it is generally unknown to them, and reveals itself in degrees over
time aside, that love is developed over many years, and partly because it has
come to dominate so much of the time spent in Lodge.
Our ritual is a necessary and valuable part of the Masonic experience,
with wisdom and teachings that continue to inspire and educate over a lifetime.
And indeed it is our ritual that makes a man a mason. Our Ritual Committee,
Inspectors and Officers Coaches teach and practice with enthusiasm and zeal,
and the results are clear in that the ritual in California is among the best and
most respected in the country.
But what of those brethren who do not have an affinity or an aptitude
for the memorization and presentation skills so necessary to our initiative
process?
Indeed, many Lodges have reinvented themselves in their member’s
image. Offering a wide variety of programs and activities that involve all the
members and often their families.
But we are again on the cusp of a large growth in membership. Not a
month goes by that there isn’t some new television “expose,” historical
representation, movie or book capitalizing on the public’s interest in
Freemasonry. And our Lodges have been capitalizing on this interest, through
Lodge web sites, Information Programs and community involvement.
But are we prepared for the new interest and growth that we have so
long awaited?
The average Brother who accedes to the Oriental Chair is generally of
exceptional intelligence, but their intellect and qualification in the ritual does
not guarantee the leadership experience necessary to deal with the challenges

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

145

and interests of a lodge and its members today. Leadership training has been
generally overlooked.
We are carpenters and mechanics, computer programmers, public
servants and retirees. And sometimes we are managers or entrepreneurs who
have some background in leadership, experience that may help in developing a
vision for the future of the lodge and motivating our officers.
Many of our lodges have internal training mechanisms to ensure our
leaders gain a variety of experience in lodge programs and management as they
progress through the chairs. But the Lodge line of officers is not the multi-year
commitment it once was. Often the new Junior Warden has recently been raised,
and can be intimidated by the challenge of writing his first article for the Trestle
Board, because his vocation or personal experience may have never required
that skill.
We are experiencing a new renaissance in the craft, with members
joining in record numbers, we must be poised to fulfill the promise of
Freemasonry for these new and enthusiastic brethren. We cannot leave it to
chance.
When it was announced that Steve Jobs was returning to the
Chairmanship of Apple Computer, their stock tripled overnight. Not because he
was a good manager, but because he was known as a great leader. Jack Welsh at
General Electric, Lee Iacocca at Chrysler, are not known as great managers, but
great leaders who have a vision of where to take their companies. Even in our
current candidates for the US Presidency we look not for management but for
leadership and a vision of where we are headed and how they will take us there.
Leaders are often made rather than born. We promise to provide those
skills to our brethren, but are we fulfilling that promise?
Our Grand Lodge recognized that Leadership training is one of its core
obligations to our Lodges, and while the Lodge Management Certification
Programs conducted throughout the state and on-line, and our Leadership
training through the Warden’s Leadership Retreats have received universal
acclaim, only half of all Lodges in California participate in these programs.
Woody Allen said that “80 percent of success is showing up. We know
this is true because that is how many of us have come to be here, we showed up,
had an aptitude for ritual or an interest in the education or fraternity of the
lodge, and someone said, “here take my seat, there’s not much to it”. The next
thing we knew, we are entrusted with the care and future of our beloved Lodge.
But what are we willing to do now that the responsibility of the Lodge
is entrusted to our care?
What skills do we expect our Lodge leaders to express and what do we
look for in Leadership for our Lodges?

146

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

We need them to understand Freemasonry’s greater purpose and
develop a vision of where they want their Lodge to go and develop a plan to
reach that vision or goal. They must have a full understanding of the
membership’s needs and desires, not just the 10 to 20 percent that attend
regularly, but the desires and interest of all the members, particularly new
members who have made significant effort to become a Brother, and have
expectations that their interest and effort will not be in vain. They need to know
what programs work, which ones don’t, and yet be willing to try new ideas.
Too many of us take pride in the Craft, not for anything we have done
or contributed, but because of the achievements and successes of others in by
gone generations.
We brag about our historical Masonic heroes and celebrities. We pat
each other on the back and tell ourselves how well we are doing, we give
ourselves grandiose titles that mean nothing to our outside friends or anyone
else, and the light of Freemasonry remains hidden within the walls of our
Lodges, never to enlighten those who would knock on the door.
And all too often we are said to be “Drinking from wells that we didn’t
dig and eating the fruit from trees that we didn’t plant”.
Many lodges have significant investments and endowments. While
some members feel that the membership themselves have an obligation to
support the Lodge, without relying on previous donations or investments, many
Masonic Halls and Centers are paid for, by the toil and efforts of those who
have come before us. Although I doubt that many deceased members left a
financial legacy to their Lodge in order to underwrite their successor’s dues or
provide discounted dinners, if that is what the Lodge decides to do, at least they
should be doing it with their eyes open.
We expect that our leaders will have a complete understanding of the
finances of the Lodge, where the money is coming from and where it is going.
Only then can they ensure that their Lodge will be a going concern for future
generations.
We need them to be trustworthy and inspire others to service. Knowing
where the talent in the lodge lies, and where it may be lacking, developing
future officers and leaders, motivating them through leadership and example, is
crucial to the long term and continued success of the Lodge.
And they must fully understand the teachings and meanings of our
symbolism and degrees. They must instill a culture of Masonic Education in all
the workings of the Lodge.
Conrad Hahn, the Masonic poet and scholar once observed “The lack
of educational work in the average lodge is the principal reason for the lack of
interest and the consequence of poor attendance in Masonry over which
spokesman have been wringing their hands for at least a century”. We must

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

147

teach the meanings of the degrees, the often lost symbolism that has been
removed from our lectures and ceremonies over the years. We must foster and
feed the curiosity that brings new members to our door.
It is often said that one should “lead, follow or get out of the way”.
Hence many of our experienced lodge leaders disappear after the installation of
their successor. It is this leadership and experience that our Lodges now require,
by developing Degree Teams to assist the Officers with an overwhelming influx
of applicants. By sharing previous successes and challenges and participating in
the education of new members and leading social and community programs.
Each of us has a responsibility to steer our fraternity into the future,
calling on our own spirit rather than deferring to those of the past. We must,
each of us, celebrate and foster the same leadership that we admire and celebrate
in our heritage. Only then can we fulfill the promise of Freemasonry.
Respectfully submitted,
Mark N. Gibson, Grand Orator

I would like to acknowledge and credit several sources of inspiration and information
used in this Oration: Norman B. Leeper, President of the Southern California Research
Lodge 2004, Jim Collins book “Good to Great”, Robert Davis book “Understanding
Manhood in America”, Cheryl Gidley's "Tenets of Leadership" and Right Worshipful
Larry L. Adamson, Chairman of the Leadership Development Committee 2004-2005.
