Medical Advisory Committee
By Gary Charland, Chairman
The Medical Advisory Committee oversees the quality of care of the
Senior Services on both campus, Covina and Union City. Covina has an
average census of 76 residents.
The committee continues to monitor quality indicators for delivery of
care. These are analyzed to show trends or patterns and are compared to
National and State benchmarks, where applicable. The indicators include: falls,
with special emphasis on significant injuries (fractures, lacerations requiring
sutures, falls requiring hospitalization), weight loss, skin conditions and
psychotropic medications. Following a comprehensive assessment of each
resident's functional capabilities, nationally established quality measures are
used to identify residents who either have or are at risk for specific functional
problems needing further evaluation . The data is evaluated monthly and
reported quarterly. National and state benchmarks for each quality measure are
used exclusively for comparison of Skilled Nursing data. Quality measures are
also evaluated in the Independent and Assisted Living levels. In the absence of
established benchmarks and documented industry standards in these areas, the
data is compared to previous quarters to identify trends or patterns that require
specific attention and intervention. Union City data reflects a good performance
with no trends or patterns identified.
Resident’s Activities of Daily Living (ADLs) and Instrumental
Activities of Daily Living (IADLs) are tracked in the RCFE and Skilled Nursing
to evaluate the amount of assistance the residents are requiring. Covina has an
average of 37 Independent residents and 28 Assisted Living residents.

226

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

The Primary Care Physicians continue to see the residents in their
private offices for the Union City Residents. This has changed the focus of the
Health Center to transporting residents to the Physician offices and has
increased the number of employees required to provide the transportation. There
is an average of 30 residents per day with scheduled medical appointments.
During the last seven months there has been a study of transportation and the
wait time residents experience after calling for pick up from the Physician
office. The outcome has been a reduction from 40 minutes to an average wait
time of 15 minutes.
In Covina, Inter-Valley Health Plan HMO has implemented a new
program for seniors called Care Level Management. Care Level Management
provides the chronically ill patient population with access to the highest quality
physician care in the home, instead of the emergency room and/or hospital. The
personal visiting physician helps reduce the cost of health care.
The certification process, for the Union City Lorber Skilled Nursing
Facility (SNF), has been completed. The provider number has an effective date
of October 12, 2007. With the initiation of Medicate Part A certification, a
monthly triple check meeting has been established. The goal of this brief
meeting is to assure the completion and accuracy of all documentation to reduce
the risk of receiving denials. The participants of this meeting include
representatives from Finance, Nursing, Rehab and Health Information
Management.
Opportunities to provide skilled nursing care for Masonic Home
residents of Covina and MOS clients through admissions to the Union City SNF
are being actively pursued. Since January 2008, there have been five transfers
from Covina and six admissions from MOS. Covina continues to contract with
four high quality skilled nursing facilities for those resident who choose to
remain near the Covina campus.
This year a decision was made to bring Rehabilitation Services inhouse at Union City to assure the Masonic Home standard for delivery of the
highest quality of care to all the residents. As a result of that decision, an
optional services survey for outpatient services took place August 17, 2007.
This survey allows the Masonic Home to bill for Medicare Part B services that
are provided to residents in the Independent and Assisted Living areas as well as
in the SNF. Although this survey had no bearing on the SNF certification it was
critical to completing the transition of Rehab Services to an in-house program.
Effective November 1, 2007 the transition of Rehabilitation services was
successfully completed.
MediCare Part D plans are selected by our Pharmacist for all residents
without qualified plans. This selection is assessed each year to assure coverage
of the maximum number of medication in each resident’s drug regimen thereby
lowering the drug costs. Several residents who have Medi-Cal coverage meet

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

227

the criteria to be designated as receiving full or partial low income subsidy. This
designation allows the pharmacy to enroll them as a participant in one of various
pharmaceutical company-sponsored Patient Assistance Programs. Patient
assistant programs by drug companies are used to provide free medications for
seniors with a low income.
It has been noted that more residents are moving into the MediCare D
coverage gap (donut hole) earlier than in the previous year. When a resident
falls into this coverage gap the Masonic Home absorbs 100% of these resident’s
prescription co-pays until the out-of-pocket prescription expenses reach a total
of $5100. Once the resident emerges from the donut hole they enter the
catastrophic stage and MediCare resumes coverage at 95% of the drug cost. The
pharmacy is closely monitoring the expense of those residents who have reached
the “donut hole” and the impact on the budget.
There has been a variety of opportunities to participate in educational
and research studies with State and Nationally known organizations at both the
Covina and Union City campuses. One very exciting 3-year study in Union City
that is being conducted through California State University – East Bay (CSUEast Bay) involves the use of the computer to assist residents with new
strategies to improve their memory. CSU- East Bay has screened approximately
58 residents and treated 17 residents over the past 2 years.
A second opportunity in Union City was a pilot study for Licensed
Vocational Nurses (LVN) Leadership. Aging Services of California in
collaboration with the University of Michigan offered this study. Union City
Skilled Nursing was one of two organizations in selected to participate. The
goal of the study was to develop and empower LVNs in leadership and
management of CNAs. The Covina campus is a training site for the East San
Gabriel Occupational Program and Technical Center. Certified Nursing
Assistance and Home Health Aides are trained to provide in-home care to
patients.
Union City staff was invited to participate in a focus group with the
California Coalition of Compassionate Care (CCCC). The goal was to assist in
the development of consumer-friendly materials on two difficult, yet common,
health care decisions faced by nursing home residents regarding life-sustaining
treatment. The two life-sustaining treatment decisions, resuscitation and
artificial nutrition and hydration, are particularly challenging. The tools
developed will cover all aspects critical to decision-making in the nursing home
setting, including explanation of the intervention, potential risks, survival rates
and how to document a resident’s wishes.
Both Covina and Union City staff took part in programs focusing on
Hospice Care, End of Life Issues, and Death and Dying. Three staff from Union
City attended a 2-day Train the Trainer conference by the End-of-Life Nursing
Education Consortium (ELNEC) Geriatrics Training Program. These

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

228

individuals will be able to further the train other staff as well as take the lead in
enhancing the End-of-Life Program of Masonic Homes.
Strategic Planning Committee
By Mark Gibson
In November 2006, the Board of Trustees adopted a three-year
strategic plan for the Masonic Homes of California. This strategic plan guided
and directed the efforts of the Trustees and staff since that time. Many of the
initiatives set forth are multi-year projects and still in progress. The strategic
plan includes three strategic imperatives that set forth the foundation for our
activities. These imperatives frame and direct our activities today:



Protecting and furthering the mission of the Masonic Homes of
California;



Protecting and optimizing the financial resources of the organization;
and,



Positioning the organization to respond to the changing and emerging
needs of the membership.

THE PROCESS
Our strategic planning process is comprehensive and involves research
into the latest thinking in senior and children’s services as well as social and
market trends that are impacting the lives of our members – today and in the
future. Most importantly, we devote significant time in hearing directly from our
membership about their needs and desires. In the development of the 2006
strategic plan and in the subsequent two years, we have conducted multiple
focus groups and surveys and ongoing research to learn about the needs and
desires of our membership.

OUR CHALLENGES
As an organization, we must identify a sustainable, long-term funding
strategy for the organization. To this end, a key component of our strategic plan
is to develop new revenue producing strategies.

FRONTAGE PROPERTY AT UNION CITY
A significant strategic opportunity with potentially strong economic
and service benefits to our organization is currently being pursued by this

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

229

committee. In March of 2007 CB Richards completed a Highest and Best Use
real estate study to realize the value of approximately 65 acres of surplus
undeveloped frontage property at the Union City campus. It considered several
key factors including: ground lease issues, physical characteristics, compatibility
with the Masonic Homes, planning and political issues, market conditions, and
financial return.
Given the economic and demographic conditions, the study concluded
that there was sufficient market for multi-family rental housing, and limited
retail demand. It further acknowledged that some portion of the property would
need to be dedicated for community benefit. Following the study, the Board of
Trustees approved a business plan and entitlement strategy to amend the
General Plan of Union City and rezone the frontage property to accommodate
mixed use development.
The entitlement strategy will include opinion
research, a ballot measure, community outreach, and CEQA compliance. The
ballot measure will require a vote of the citizens of Union City at the 2010
general election. At this time the professional consulting team is being
organized to begin the campaign work for a successful election.

THE ACACIA CREEK COMMUNITIES
After several years of research, marketing, and development work, the
Acacia Creek Communities are moving forward to become a reality. The
Masonic Homes of California is the sole corporate member of AC Covina and
AC Union City. The new senior living communities are being developed on
unused surplus land at the existing campuses and will serve approximately 400
fraternal members. Additionally the projects will generate new revenue streams
to support the charitable needs of the Masonic Homes through land leases and
management fees. Acacia Creek at Covina is scheduled to break ground in
November 2008 and will include 112 new independent living residences.
Acacia Creek at Union City broke ground in September of 2007 and includes
152 new independent living residences. On both campuses, residents will be
offered a variety of floor plans– ranging from one bedroom units of 845 sq feet
to two bedrooms with den cottages of 1650 square feet.
The communities will include new dining rooms, indoor and outdoor
pools, a variety of recreational spaces and separate Assisted Living and Memory
Support care. This committee invested a significant amount of time completing
the initial extensive feasibility and market studies. We are gratified to see how
favorably the membership is responding to this concept. This initiative is now
being directed by the Acacia Creek Board of Directors. We are grateful and
appreciative of their service. They are

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Larry Adamson, President
Mark Gibson
George Geanoulis

Ara Maloyan

to Larry L. Adamson for his years of service as Founding President of the Board

MASTER PLANNING
During this reporting period the Strategic Planning Committee directed
staff to initiate an update to the Masonic Homes of California campus master
plans that were dated 2002. The update is focused on meeting the future needs
of Masonic Homes of California and addressing the shared spaces between
Masonic Homes and Acacia Creek. For the Covina campus the plans include
the possibility of providing additional affordable housing to meet the charitable
needs of the fraternity. The plans also include improving the entrance to the site
and redesigning the conference and meeting space on campus. At the Union
City campus the plans include addressing the need for additional parking, the
adequacy of space for the auditorium and pharmacy and a review of the
appropriate number of units needed for the higher levels of care. The Strategic
Planning Committee plans to finish this report in the coming year.

MASONIC OUTREACH SERVICES (MOS)
Our MOS program has grown significantly over the past three years –
in fact we’ve seen a growth of over 25%, serving approximately 155 clients per
month. During this period of time, we’ve restructured our programs and
services to accomplish the following:



Developed a new centralized intake department to streamline and
improve the admission policies for all of our communities



Created procedures for serving those in need of Assisted Living
through community partnerships



Created a new case management program for those needing help
accessing necessary services (without needing financial support)



Accessing all forms of available reimbursement to reduce the cost per
case

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA



231

Increased emphasis on lodge and family involvement in the care and
support of our MOS clients

CHILDREN’S SERVICES
In addition to identifying and responding to the strategic initiatives for
our senior programs, the Children’s Program Committee and the Strategic
Planning Committee have launched an intensive strategic planning process for
children and family services. The monthly average of children served is 34 in
residential care and another 140 clients through the Family Resource Center’s
outreach program. Our goal is to build on the strengths of our current program,
ensure that our services reflect the changing needs of our fraternity, and make
the best and highest use of our services. The committees also approved new
operational initiatives, including a new staffing model and the hiring of a
Clinical Director. This committee would like to acknowledge and thank the
members of the Children’s Program Committee for their committed and
sensitive oversight of this program.

CONCLUSION
Preserving and protecting our charitable mission and providing the best
possible care to our residents and clients is the primary focus of the Strategic
Planning Committee of Board of Trustees of the Masonic Homes of California.
We are committed to creating a long-term, sustainable financial plan for the
organization that ensures the continuity and perpetuity of our historical mission.
In closing, the Strategic Planning Committee and its Chairman would like to
acknowledge the leadership and support of the entire Board of Trustees of the
Masonic Homes and its President, Allen B. Gresham, PGM. Together we are
committed to upholding the standards of excellence demanded of us by the
guiding principles of freemasonry and to ensure our services are delivered in a
manner befitting our founding ideals. It is an honor and a privilege to serve as a
member of this Board.

President’s Report
By Allen B. Gresham
It has been a busy year at the Masonic Homes characterized by great
service, exciting changes and the achievement of important milestones. In
August, we hired a new Executive Vice President, Melvin Matsumoto to
oversee our operations and guide our path to the future. Mel is a nationally
recognized leader in the senior living industry with a long and distinguished
career devoted to the service of the most vulnerable members of our society.
His experience and his commitment to our mission assured us that he was the

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

232

right person to lead this important institution during this critical time. To
further support the efforts of the organization, we have restructured our
executive team:


Rob Fallon was named Executive Director of Senior Services over both
the Union City and Covina campuses.



Judy Figueroa was named the Administrator of the Covina campus.



John Howl was named Vice President of Strategic Development for the
organization.



Steffani Kizziar was named Executive Director of Outreach and
Children’s Services.



Dixie Reeve was named Administrator of the Union City campus.

The service provided through the Masonic Homes is done in the name,
and on the behalf, of every Mason in California. In the name of freemasonry,
this year we have served over 330 members of our fraternal family in our
residential communities; 165 through our Masonic Outreach Program; 46
children in our residential community and 244 families through our Family
Resource Center.
Most importantly, all of these individuals have been served in a manner
befitting our founding ideals. In the resident satisfaction survey conducted this
year we received an overall rating of 96%, which places in the top 5% of our
industry.
In addition to providing financial support to members in need, the
Masonic Outreach Services program is now providing case management
services. These services are designed to meet the needs of those who don’t
require financial support but do need help accessing appropriate supportive
services. The MOS program is larger than ever before in its history and helps
each one of us fulfill our Masonic obligations to our brothers and their widows.
We received a phenomenal 98% satisfaction rate on a recent survey of
the clients and families receiving support through our Masonic Outreach
Services (MOS) department. Not only did our members complete the survey,
they wrote page after page of comments about the life-saving impact of our
support. Our program and services are best described in the words of one of
our worthy brethren: “Without you, my wife and I would not be alive. We would
be totally homeless…. I cannot describe the positive impact your support has on
us nor our endless gratitude… We never dreamed we’d need you, but thank
God you’re there!”

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

233

This is what it means when we say we provide care in the name and on
the behalf of every Mason in California. And this is what is possible through
the generosity of those who donate to the Annual Fund directed to the MOS
program. The dollars that are given are put directly to use for such members
and widows in need.
Grand Master Richard Wakefield Hopper’s Project TREE provided
funding to expand and improve the services offered through the Children’s and
Family Services program at the Masonic Home at Covina. The program’s
administrative offices relocated to a renovated home, a case manager position
was added, new parenting classes were offered and our child graduates received
intensive support as they transitioned from our residential program. Because of
the vision of our Grand Master and those who generously supported his project,
this year, the Family Resource Center provided services to almost 250 families
in need.
In addition to serving our members today, we’ve passed important
milestones on our quest to serve more members of our fraternal family in the
future. After years of planning and research, last year two new corporations
were formed - Acacia Creek at Covina and Acacia Creek at Union City. These
corporations have their own Board of Directors charged with the responsibility
of developing, funding and marketing new Senior Living Communities for our
fraternal family. At the outset of this process, they said if you came – they
would build. It is with great pride and excitement that I now report you have
come and they are building!
On August 22, 2008, the Union City campus of the Acacia Creek
Masonic Senior Living Community kicked off the vertical construction with a
Cornerstone Ceremony presided over by Most Worshipful, Richard Wakefield
Hopper. Officers of the Grand Lodge, the Masonic Homes Board of Trustees,
the Acacia Creek Board of Directors, our founders and local dignitaries also
attended. Actual construction has now begun and we are looking forward to our
Founders taking occupancy in about 18 months.
Further, we are pleased to announce that Acacia Creek at Covina will
be holding its Groundbreaking Ceremony on Sunday, November 16, 2008. In
about two years, we will be able to serve an additional 400 members of our
fraternal family. All in the name of Freemasonry.
Other highlights of our organizational performance this year included:


Conducting a comprehensive safety review of our senior and children’s
program resulting in new policies and procedures.



Updating the master plans on both campuses.

property.



Developing a new format for our strategic planning to ensure our goals
are consistent with the overall direction of our organization and
accomplished within the agreed upon timeframe.



Developing a new Board and committee structure and meeting

resources while strengthening the governance of our organization.

Serving on this Board is a tremendous responsibility and one that I, and
every Trustee, take very seriously. I here want to thank each and every member
of the Board of Trustees of the Masonic Homes of California for their great
efforts and tireless dedication to this great institution. I can say that in all that
we do, we are guided by our commitment to serving our brethren in a manner

the lives of our fraternal family are at stake. I want to close with the words of
one our brothers: “I just cannot thank you sufficiently enough for the help that
has been given to my wife and I ….You have shown the motto “Brotherly love,
relief and truth” to be a definite fact. God bless you all.”

REPORT OF THE ASSOCIATE EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR
MASONIC HOME AT UNION CITY
As new Administrator of the Masonic Home of Union City it is an
honor and privilege to represent the residents and staff in this annual report.
A primary goal and key achievement this year has been to prepare for
Medicare/Medi-Cal certification for the Lorber Skilled Nursing Facility. Five
Department of Public Health surveyors and a Life Safety survey successfully
completed the certification process in August 2007. The provider number was
effective on October 12, 2007. The Department of Public Health will do annual
surveys of Skilled Nursing on a random basis. Completion of the Medi-Cal
certification process has been followed by the establishment of a goal to
maintain the census with 40% of the residents to be covered through Medi-Cal.
Through the certification preparation process, staff, with the
participation of several residents, has accomplished a significant achievement
with the comprehensive update and revision of the Masonic Home of Union
City Fire and Disaster Plan. This included employee and resident training and
drills. The Home has 27 Community Adult Volunteers on our "Second
Responders Disaster Team." A phone tree drill was recently performed with
excellent results. These volunteers will supply support as needed to the staff and
residents.
A decision was made to eliminate the outside contract and bring
Rehabilitation Services in-house to assure the Masonic Home standard for
delivery of the highest quality of care to all the residents. As a result of that
decision, an optional services survey for outpatient services took place August
17, 2007. This survey allows the Masonic Home to bill for Medicare Part B
services that are provided to residents in the Independent and Assisted Living
areas as well as in the SNF. Although this survey had no bearing on the SNF
certification it was critical to completing the transition of Rehab Services to an
in-house program. Effective November 1, 2007 the transition of Rehabilitation
services was successfully completed.
A key achievement this year was the Siminoff Lodge UD obtaining its
constitution thereby becoming a Charter Lodge. Siminoff Lodge was formed on
campus in October 2007. The Lodge began in response to an idea under the
leadership, motivation, and guidance of Virgil Land, a coach of the Lodge. The
following Master Masons are active in the Lodge: Doc Adamson, Alex
Adorador, Sterling Bailey, Williams Beers, Bill Bergdahl, John Bull, Fred
Clodfelter, Mort Collis, Charles Cox, Allan Davidson, George Dolfin, Irwin
Fershleiser, Mitch Franceschini, Bill Fretz, Syd Furman, Steve Gassett, James
Gloeckler, Jesse Gomez, Jack Goodwin, Roger Gurley, Harold Harrison, Wally
Heglund, Ellsworth Herr, Woody Hibbs, Bob Huntington, Harry Innis, Carlton
Ivey, Mike Kilbury, Richard Kirkman, Virgil Land, Jim Leggate, Ralph Loomis,

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

237

Bob McCain, Jack McClellan, Bill McNerney, Fred Meyers, Arbie Pierson,
Chuck Riise, Ray Rosson, Chuck Silverman, Clifford Smith, Leonard Smith,
Lauryn Taylor, William Teuteberg, Jack Tomlinson, George Turegano and
Keith Wong.
The Masonic Garden Club, under the watchful eye of John Marshall,
Director of Dining Services, continues to be a great addition for residents to dig
in and grow their own vegetables. The club has expanded beyond the initial
location on Second Adams to an area below the Administrator’s house, lovingly
named the ‘North Forty’. This area is comprised of 2/3 of an acre of land and in
used to grow approximately 300 tomato plants, 12 rows of sweet corn,
watermelon, honeydew, cucumbers, onions, radishes, green peas and snow peas.
The residents are tending the gardens, especially the North Forty. To the delight
and gastronomic pleasure of the Masonic Home residents, all of these fruits and
vegetables are used in the planning of the Masonic Home menus.
The annual Covina trip to see the Fabulous Palm Spring Follies
production of Tin Pan Alley took place this spring. Sixteen Union City residents
visited Covina for a two night stay. Several new residents who have never been
to the Covina campus were included. It was a nostalgic visit as the buildings the
residents have been lodged in over the years will be demolished to make way
for construction on the Covina campus. The Covina and Union City staff
worked well together to insure all the resident needs were met. Fourteen Covina
residents joined the Union City group for the Follies. The residents had a
wonderful time visiting friends in Covina and bridging the gap the miles create.
The hospitality of our Covina friends has been deeply appreciated.
To their benefit, Union City residents and staff have had multiple
opportunities to participate in educational and research studies with a variety of
State and Nationally known organizations. One very exciting 3-year study is
being conducted through California State University – East Bay (CSU-East
Bay) by Associate Professor Nidhi Mahendra. This research, granted by the
Alzheimer’s Association, involves the use of the computer to assist resident
with new strategies to improve their memory. CSU- East Bay has screened
approximately 58 residents and treated 17 residents over the past 2 years.
Another study involved the chance for the licensed staff of the Skilled
Nursing Facility to participate in a pilot study for Licensed Vocational Nurses
(LVN) Leadership. Aging Services of California in collaboration with the
University of Michigan offered this study. Union City Skilled Nursing was one
of two organizations selected to participate. The goal of the study was to
develop and empower LVNs in leadership and management of CNAs.
A third opportunity was an invitation to participate in a focus group
with the California Coalition of Compassionate Care (CCCC). The goal was to
develop consumer-friendly materials on two difficult, yet common, health care
decisions faced by nursing home residents regarding life-sustaining treatment.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

238

The two life-sustaining treatment decisions, resuscitation and artificial nutrition
and hydration, are particularly challenging. The tools developed will cover all
aspects critical to decision-making in the nursing home setting, including
explanation of the intervention, potential risks, and survival rates and how to
document a resident’s wishes.
A major impact on the community has been the construction of the new
Acacia Creek Community. Demolition of the Acacia buildings and the barns
began in August of 2007. This drew quite of crowd of residents to watching the
event. The ground breaking ceremony was held on October 7, 2007. Masonic
Homes Residents were invited as well as many others. Acacia Creek has a live
webcast on their website (www.acaciacreek.org) for all to watch the progress on
the construction site. Since some of the residents don’t have computers, Acacia
Creek has installed a flat screen TV in the main dining room waiting area which
broadcasts from the website. Many residents enjoy watching the day to day
construction progress. The residents have had a number of Acacia Creek
presentations by Dan Wiley, Acacia Creek Marketing Director, so they are
aware of the new community and what it offers. A Cornerstone Ceremony is
being planned for August 22, 2008.
The Holidays bring many parties, celebrations and traditional events to
the Masonic Home of Union City. The lighting of the official Union City
Holiday Tree located in front of the Head building was done differently this
year. This is in response to the more limited parking from the Acacia Creek
project than is required by inviting the public. This year the event was opened to
only residents, employees and their families. Although this was the first time it
rained during the event, the lighting was thoroughly enjoyed by all who
attended. The residents and staff chose to have the employee who had worked
here the longest, Antonia Ponce from Housekeeping, do the ceremonial lighting
of the 110 foot tree. Antonia, who has served residents at Union City for 37
years, was deserving of the honor.
As always, volunteers have been very busy on campus this year. There
were 190 volunteers involved in the set up and take down of the twenty-four
Holiday Trees.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT FROM THE EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR
OF OUTREACH AND CHILDREN’S SERVICES
Masonic Outreach Services
This year our Masonic Outreach Services (MOS) department served
more individuals than ever before in our history. Over 200 vulnerable members
of our fraternal family who live throughout the state received financial support
and/or case management services.
We strengthened our case management services to ensure that all those
on the waiting list for either one of our senior living communities received the
help they needed while they wait for residential services.
We were very pleased with the results of our recent satisfaction survey
in which we received an overall satisfaction rate of 98%, demonstrating that we
are serving our members in a manner consistent with our founding ideals.

Children's Services
This year we served over 45 children in our residential program. Our
children all demonstrated significant improvements in their grade point averages
and behavioral issues and are well positioned for bright futures.
Through the vision of Grand Master Richard Wakefield Hopper,
Project Tree raised money for our Family Resource Center, which allowed us to
relocate our administrative offices, add a new case manager position, offer new
parenting classes, improve our services to our child graduates and serve almost
250 families in need.
We launched an intensive strategic visioning process for children and
families services in our organization. Over the next six months, a task force will
conduct a comprehensive review of the needs of our fraternal family and
communities and define the role of the Masonic Homes in addressing those
needs.
Nine child graduates of our program are receiving scholarships for
post-graduate high school students. All of them are doing well in school and are
being closely supported by the staff of our Family Resource Center.
Respectfully submitted,
Steffani Kizziar

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

249

REPORT OF THE ADMINISTRATION
AND CAMPUS HEALTH SERVICES
On behalf of the residents and staff of the Masonic Homes at Covina,
we are pleased to present the following Annual Report highlighting the
successes and challenges of the past year. As you read our report we hope you
will sense our continued commitment to excellence as we serve our residents
and families. We would like to also express our sincere appreciation to the
Board of Trustees and our Masonic Lodges for their dedication and faithful
support of our community.
During the past year we served a senior population which averaged 76
residents and represented a resident mix of 48% Independent Living, 35%
Assisted Living and 13% Skilled Nursing. Over the same period, we had 22
admissions, 5 transfers to our Union City campus and 5 deaths.
Our Waiting List included 35 prospective residents representing 28
households. The average waiting period for admission was approximately 36
months. Our staff is committed to call each prospective applicant monthly to
inquire how they are doing both physically and financially. Referrals to our
Masonic Outreach Program are made if services and support are needed.
Our Senior Community offers a “Continuing Care” model of care that
provides a full spectrum of care and services. Each resident is treated with
respect and dignity as they “age in place” in a safe and caring environment. Our
campus does not have a skilled nursing facility on site. To support the skilled
nursing needs of our residents, we have established special transfer
arrangements with our Union City campus and with four high quality skilled
nursing facilities in our surrounding community for those residents who would
like to remain near the Covina campus. Although our residents reside off
campus, we remain involved in the oversight of their care. Our staff and
residents visit the skilled nursing facilities regularly. We conduct routine quality
reviews to assure high quality of care for our residents.
In further focusing on the health and safety of our residents, we
continue to monitor and measure quality indicators which include falls,
unexpected weight changes, hospital and emergency room visits, changes in
Activity of Daily Living and Incidental Activities of Daily Living. By tracking
this data we are better able to identify trends and monitor changes in residents’
health. Our findings also enable us to improve the effectiveness of our resident
needs and service planning process.
In April of 2008, we conducted our Annual Resident Satisfaction
Survey. For the fifth consecutive year our overall resident satisfaction survey
scores were well above Holleran’s national benchmark. We had 100% of our
residents participating in the survey. The overall satisfaction scores for

250

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Independent Living were 92.7 and 96 for Assisted Living. Both scores were
above Holleran’s national benchmark 86.2 of 87.5 respectively. Our top
performing strengths include safety and security of apartments, buildings and
grounds, friendliness and courtesy of staff, variety and quality of suitable
resident activities, confidence in emergency medical response and
approachability of administration regarding questions and concerns.
Through feedback from our many resident committees, we have
developed and implemented action plans focused on areas in library services,
dining services and housekeeping services. As a team of staff we have adopted a
custom service culture and continue to implement our Masonic Homes Service
Excellence programs. This specially designed program reflects the Masonic
Values of Brotherly love, Relief and Truth and teaches customer service skills to
all our staff.
Our campus is proud to be the training site for two new programs. The
East San Gabriel Occupational and Technical program and the Masters in Social
Work program of USC. The senior side of our campus provides in home patient
care training for Certified Nurses Assistants who are furthering their careers in
home health care. Both the seniors’ and children’s’ programs provide training in
Social Work.
One of the unique attributes of our community is that we serve both
children and seniors on our campus. We have a wonderful Intergenerational
Program. The seniors and children spend quality time together at events both on
and off campus. We also have an adopted Grandparent Program where each
children’s’ home has one or more senior residents as adopted Grandparents.
On June 8, 2008 our campus enjoyed our 3rd successful Summer
Festival. Our Summer Festival joins together our Masonic Lodges and affiliated
Masonic bodies for a time of fellowship, great food, entertainment and fun. Our
theme this year was “Take Me Out to the Ballgame.”
During the summer months, our staff and residents are busy hosting
BBQs and providing campus tours for our local Masonic Lodges, affiliated
groups and organizations. The seniors and children have the opportunity to
interact with our guests and show off their homes.
On July 29, 2008 we held our Annual Volunteer Luncheon. We gave
recognition to 73 volunteers for a total of 6180 hours of service this past year.
Many of our senior residents, R.O.P. students, Interns and outside community
residents actively volunteer along with several residents from our children’s’
community.
This year we have completed several significant capital projects to
improve our living environment and the quality of life for our residents. The
project list includes; replacing heating and air conditioning units in the
residents’ apartments; installation of mixing valves on water heaters for the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

251

residents to maintain hot water temperature; re-carpeting the residents’ Health
Center and Assisted Living dining room and the replacement of vehicles for
facility use.
As a resident centered, mission driven community, we are dedicated
and committed to carry out our mission to provide quality care to California
Master Masons, their wives, widows and mothers, as well as to children within
and without Masonic affiliations, with dignity and compassion within an
atmosphere based on Masonic tenants, that fulfill the physical, social, spiritual
and nurturing needs of our extended community.
Respectfully submitted
Judy Figueroa

257

REPORT OF THE TRUSTEES OF THE
CALIFORNIA MASONIC FOUNDATION
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.&A.M. of California:
The Foundation Trustees are pleased to once more report a year of
growth in supporting the Strategic Plan of our Grand Lodge. Key items include
our outreach to the youth of California, to the teachers of our public school
system, and to the membership of our Fraternity.
Commitment to Youth and Public Education
SAP Conference
In February 2008, your California Masonic Foundation and the
California Department of Education co-sponsored an educational
conference held in our California Masonic Memorial Temple. SAP is the
acronym for Student Assistance Program, a large umbrella of programs
including our Masonic Student Assistance Program. The overall focus
remains on training core teams of teachers to identify and help youth in
their schools that may be at-risk in a number of different ways. This firstof-its-kind event delivered two days of information and training for more
than 400 attendees from throughout California. The conference began with
a DVD presentation from State Superintendent of Education, Jack
O’Connell.
This conference is another example of the growing relationship
between your Foundation and the State Department of Education. In
addition to the SAP conference, your Foundation continues to be a primary
sponsor of the California Teacher of the Year program, with our Grand
Master invited to the award presentation in Sacramento each February.

MSAP Training and Fundraising Update
In late February, the final MSAP training of the 2007-2008 school
year was held in Union City at our Masonic Home facility. This training
session was the largest MSAP class in our history, with more than 120
teachers and administrators in attendance.
For the 2007-2008 school year, your Foundation supported the
training of 735 teachers, administrators, and other school personnel, from
132 different California schools. This represents the most trained in any
year in fourteen year history of MSAP in our Jurisdiction. Your
Foundation proudly invested $316,000 in this program this past year, and
we look forward to another productive year for 2008-2009.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

258

We also extend special recognition to the leadership of Vesper
Lodge #84 in Red Bluff. They have displayed their belief in the importance
of MSAP by both their leadership level monetary support of our program,
as well as bringing MSAP training to their community this past February.
[Please join me in thanking them.]

Scholarship Program
The Foundation began the 2008-2009 school year once again
committed to our exemplary scholarship program. Since its inception in
1969, your California Masonic Foundation has awarded tens of millions of
dollars in scholarships to deserving California high school seniors. The
criteria have always included a demonstrated financial need and superior
academic performance. We also provide a preference to those applicants
with a Masonic affiliation and/or participation in our Masonic Youth
Orders.
This year, your Foundation awarded nine new $40,000 C.E.
Towne Scholarships, and forty-three new $2,500 scholarships to deserving
California high school seniors. Totaling nearly $1,000,000, scholarships
this year were awarded to more than 200 individual students. Because of
our support, these young men and women are matriculating to top tier fouryear colleges and universities across the country. They are often leaders in
their schools, and we believe will become leaders in our future.

Cornerstone Ceremony Grants
In the year ending on June 30, 2008, your Foundation is pleased to
report the award of seventeen $1,000 Cornerstone Grants to brand new
schools where the Grand Master was invited to perform our traditional
Cornerstone Ceremony. These grants are without restriction, and can be
used as the principal sees fit. The awards punctuate our commitment to
education as well as provide a community an opportunity to see Masons at
work!

Child I.D. Program
With the continuing generosity of the Craft, and the hard work of
local Lodge members, this program remains a linchpin of our community
outreach. The Child ID Committee continues to provide the necessary
equipment, supplies, and training throughout our Jurisdiction.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

259

Leadership Training for Youth Groups
Your Foundation remains pleased to provide funding for
leadership training of our upcoming leaders.
During this Annual
Communication each group will be presented by the Grand Master a grant
check for their training costs.

Commitment to Fraternal Education and Leadership
Masonic Education and Leadership Training
For the past four years, your Foundation has raised annual funds
from our membership in support of Masonic Education and Leadership
Training (MELT) programs. In the year ending June 30th, we raised nearly
$200,000 for these education and leadership training programs. The
funding provides support for important Masonic programs, including the
Wardens Retreats, Lodge Management Certification Program, and the
California Masonic Symposium. These are programs which make our
Jurisdiction one of the best in the world. Without your support, some of
these programs simply could not happen.
Our annual Symposium, held this year in our California Masonic
Memorial Temple, was again a success. Nearly 100 Masons and guests
came together for a topical discourse concerning Women and Freemasonry.
California continues to be a leader among Grand Lodges in contributing to
the international conversation about Freemasonry, its history, and its future.

Commitment to Making the World a Better Place
Your Foundation board has taken to heart this guidance from Fred
Miller, a noted consultant to non-profit boards: “Strategically thinking boards
and executives focus on questions, consider alternative scenarios, are
comfortable with ambiguity and uncertainty, and use much of their time
together for collaborative thinking. The result is a thinking and learning
process characterized by a commitment to core beliefs and a capacity to grow,
adapt, and act strategically.” This means that we no longer look only inward,
but stronger than ever extending outward the gentle hand of Freemasonry as one
solution to the myriad of issues which affect families, schools, and communities
of this great State of California.
This Grand Lodge supports public education as a prime virtue, and
encourages each Lodge to likewise support their local students, teachers, and
schools. Your Foundation works diligently to also add value and meaning
beyond our scholarship program. For example, we bring together teachers,
administrators, and Lodges in rural areas together with our MSAP training. We

260

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

partner with the Department of Education to bring statewide issues to light; the
SAP Conference being an example. Currently, we are meeting with large urban
school districts to distill common needs of students in populous areas, where we
also find large concentrations of Masons. These ongoing meetings continue to
strengthen the relationship between your Foundation, our local Lodges, and
those charged with educating our youth.
As we serve the constituents of your Foundation, we necessarily bring
together disparate groups that have otherwise remained at a distance. In
performing this role, we have realized that we often serve as conveners, creating
mutual purpose without personal gain. However, as a strategic-thinking board,
we are also at times funders, organizers, leaders, fundraisers, and at all times
Masons. As cheerleaders, we celebrate as a fraternity the good works done by
our brethren in their own communities, in whatever capacity, as we are all but
one family.
To extend the helping hand of 60,000 Masons, lifting up those in need,
and providing wise counsel for the common good are but part of our
contribution in making the world a better place.
Respectfully Submitted,
John F. Lowe, President
Myron A. Tisdel, Vice-President
Thomas T. Albers
David D. Johnson
Thomas E. Kurth

261

REPORT OF THE BOARD OF TRUSTEES
OF THE CALIFORNIA MASONIC MEMORIAL TEMPLE
Most Worshipful Grand Master and members of the Grand Lodge:
This afternoon I am pleased to make the 54th annual report of the
California Masonic Memorial Temple since its incorporation in 1954.
The CMMT owns this great edifice in which we meet today and
derives tax-exempt revenue by leasing its space to the Grand Lodge of
California whose offices are on the third floor; to the Nob Hill Masonic Center
that operates the auditorium and garage; and to other third parties who lease
space in the building. We also receive tax-exempt contributions from
candidates who are initiated into California lodges. We are very happy that this
number exceeded 1,900 new initiates this year. With these revenue streams, the
expenses for operating and maintaining this building were paid for this year
with some additional funds left over to expense depreciation as we look to
funding necessary improvements in the future.

Endomosaic Mural Restoration
Our primary capital project this year was the restoration of the
Endomosaic mural that adorns our lobby and is the center piece of the building.
Created by Big Sur artist Emile Norman more than 50 years ago, ours is the
largest Endowmosaic mural in the world and the most significant work of the
artist. After 50 years of existence, the mural was in great need of restoration.
The lapse of time and the affects of the elements caused the panels to come
apart and the materials within (glass, plastic, and organic materials) were
slipping and falling to the bottom on the various panels. The panels suffered
from cracks and discoloration. Using the talents of Architectural Resources
Group, California’s premiere authority in architectural preservation, each of the
45 panels were carefully restored earlier this year and today the mural shines
and sparkles nearly as well as it did when it was first installed 50 years ago.
This restoration was completed at a cost of approximately $450,000. I
am proud to report that as of today, over $400,000 of that cost has been paid for
by donations from our members. Last night we had the opportunity to recognize
those donors in the presence of the artist and those who restored the mural.
Now an exhibit is available on the mezzanine which explains the significance of
the mural and how it is constructed. The exhibit also pays tribute to the artist
and recognizes the donors who made the restoration possible. I hope you have
the opportunity to view that exhibit this weekend, if you have not already.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

262

Upgrade of the Auditorium and California Room
A good deal of our efforts this year has been devoted to developing a
plan to keep our facility viable for the future and competitive in the ever
changing market in which we operate. Working with several business partners,
we will embark this year on a plan that will optimize the utilization of this
building, enable us to meet our current and future operating needs, and provide
the means for the increasing costs of maintenance, repair, and restoration. This
plan will include improvements in this Auditorium and the California Room.
The improvements will make the spaces more competitive and will better serve
our own needs for the Annual Communication. We look forward to sharing
these upgrades with you next year.
50th Anniversary of the CMMT Dedication
Of course, we are especially proud this year to celebrate the 50th
Anniversary of the dedication of this Memorial Temple. This is a historic time
for all of us. It is a time for us to celebrate, but also to pause and remember all
of the work by those who have come before us, which has preserved this
building as a landmark for Masonry in California. This anniversary reminds us
of our own obligation to be good stewards of this important asset. Later this
afternoon, the Grand Master and his Grand Lodge officers will formally
rededicate this building to the tenets and virtues for which it was originally
dedicated 50 years ago. Significant planning has gone into this ceremony. I
hope you are able stay for what will be a memorable occasion.
On behalf of the Board of Trustees of the California Masonic Memorial
Temple, I wish to extend our gratitude to the Grand Secretary and the staff who
work each day to maintain this building and manage its business so effectively.
We wish also to thank the Grand Master, Most Worshipful Richard Wakefield
Hopper, for the opportunity to serve in this capacity.

Myron E. Tisdel, President
Larry L. Adamson

Respectfully submitted,
David D. Johnson, Vice President
Edmund M. Lim
Mark E. Pressey

REPORT OF THE BOARD OF DIRECTORS
OF THE NOB HILL MASONIC CENTER, INC.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
The newly recreated Nob Hill Masonic Center, Inc. reported to you last
year at the Annual Communication estimated net income (before income tax) of
$112,676 for the 8½ month fiscal year 2007 –estimated because the report was
presented in September and the fiscal year did not end until October 31, 2007.
The final audited report shows $79,448 net income before taxes and $57,191 net
income after payment of federal and state income taxes for fiscal 2007. During
October 2008 a dividend of $57,125 will be paid to our sole stockholder – the
Grand Lodge of California.
During fiscal 2008, that is November 2007 through October 2008, Nob
Hill has continued to aggressively market the use of the Auditorium by outside
entities for concerts, lectures, corporate meetings, trade shows, and
Naturalization Ceremonies conducted by the Department of Homeland Security.
In addition, the management agreement covering the Parking Garage expired,
and we solicited proposals for a new agreement. Three proposals were received,
and an agreement with a new management firm was executed which resulted in
increased revenues.
Most of the employees of the former management firm were given the
opportunity of joining the new firm, and many who had been employed at our
facility for many years accepted the offer.
Income and expenses through mid July 2008 and projected income and
expenses through October 2008 are as follows:
Auditorium Revenue............................................................................ $1,271,431
Garage Revenue ................................................................................... $1,629,850
Total Revenue ...................................................................................... $2,901,280
Auditorium Expense ............................................................................ $1,337,050
Garage Expense ...................................................................................... $586,569
Rent (paid to CMMT), property taxes
& General administrative................................................................. $722,622
Total Expense ...................................................................................... $2,646,242
Net Income (before income tax) ...................................................... $255,039
Net Income (after income tax) ......................................................... $158,571

This after tax revenue should enable Nob Hill to more than double its
dividend to Grand Lodge.

264

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Nob Hill will continue to manage the Auditorium, Exhibit Hall, and
Parking Garage until December 15, 2008. At that time, as you have heard from
CMMT, the owners of the property, Nob Hill, will cease management of the
Auditorium and Exhibit Hall, but will continue to manage the Parking Garage.
Our chief operating officer, Grand Secretary, Allan L. Casalou, and his
staff continue to handle the day to day operation of this facility, and we all owe
them a debt of gratitude for their tireless efforts.
On behalf of the Board of Directors of Nob Hill Masonic Center, Inc. I
thank Most Worshipful Richard Wakefield Hopper for the opportunity to be of
service.
Respectfully submitted,
M. William Holsinger, PGM, President

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON FINANCE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Your Finance Committee serves two distinct roles for the Grand Lodge
and Related Entities: as Finance Committee for the Grand Lodge and as the
Retirement Committee. In addition, we work closely with the Audit Committee
and with individual Lodges throughout the jurisdiction to assist with financial
matters.
Sitting as the Retirement Committee, we are charged with the oversight
and general administration of the Grand Lodge and Related Entities Defined
Benefit Retirement Plan. Working closely with Grand Lodge staff, external
actuaries, independent accountants and the Investment Committee of the Grand
Lodge, the Committee assures the effective and efficient management and
funding of the retirement plan. The Defined Benefit Retirement Plan was frozen
in 2007 and replaced with a generous 401(k) plan. This is consistent with the
action taken in recent years by many employers throughout the country in an
effort to reduce expenses. The 401(k) plan has been very well received by our
employees.
The Finance Committee of your Grand Lodge reports that is has
reviewed all the Grand Master’s Recommendations and the proposed
Legislation before the Grand Lodge. None of the Grand Master’s Decisions or
Recommendations, or any of the Resolutions will have major financial impact to
the Grand Lodge budget.
The Finance Committee has thoroughly reviewed the fiscal year 20082009 budget proposed by Deputy Grand Master Larry Adamson. The proposed
budget expenses reflect an increase of just under $241,000 or 10.4% over the
prior year approved budget. The increase in energy prices is a major component
of the increase. In addition, insurance costs are expected to increase by 6.5%.
Even with this large increase in expenses, per capita is only rising 3%, from $29
to $31.
The budget also projects a continuing decline in membership over the
previous year. The Grand Lodge budget projects 61,520 members, or a
decrease of about 2,200 members. Your Grand Lodge leadership is working to
create new and exciting programs to increase the membership of the fraternity.
The Finance Committee has also reviewed the proposed per capita
request from Senior Grand Warden Kenneth Nagel. It is proposed that the per
capita for the 2009-2010 fiscal year remain unchanged at $31. If approved, this
is the first time that per capita has not increased in this jurisdiction in many
years.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

269

The Finance Committee continues to provide you and your Grand
Lodge with effective financial oversight and transparency of the financial
resources entrusted to our care. It is a pleasure to serve on your behalf.
Respectfully submitted,
Russell E. Charvonia, Chairman
Larry Becker
Robert C. Coe

Lester W. Miller
Gary C. Young
Thomas E. Kurth

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON JURISPRUDENCE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A.M. of California:
The Committee on Jurisprudence serves as the legal adviser to the
Grand Master and the legal adviser to the Grand Lodge when in session. The
Committee reviews proposed resolutions and recommendations to be considered
at the next Annual Communication and prepares any necessary legislation to
carry out the intent of the proponents. At the Annual Communication, it
comments on the legality of the Grand Master’s decisions and dispensations and
on the legal effect of the proposed recommendations and resolutions. After the
Annual Communication, it prepares the changes to the California Masonic Code
to reflect the legislation which was adopted.
In the Committee’s view, those of Grand Master Hopper’s decisions
and dispensations coming to its attention were legal and within his discretion.
In the Committee’s view, the legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on July
25th of this year is sufficient to carry out the intent of the proponents, and the
legislation contained in Grand Master Hopper’s recommendations which he will
present in his message is sufficient to carry out his intent. We believe that each
piece of this legislation has the legal effect set forth in it.
The legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on July 25th of this year
and the legislation contained in Grand Master Hopper’s recommendations
require no separate motion because they are automatically before this Annual
Communication. The presiding officer will announce the vote required for the
passage of each item before discussion begins. Any legislation which does not
amend the California Masonic Code or the Ritual requires a simple majority
only for adoption. Any amendment to the California Masonic Code or the
Ritual must be adopted by a five-sixths affirmative vote. If the amendment
receives a simple majority but less than a five-sixths affirmative vote, it carries
over to the next Annual Communication when only a two-thirds affirmative vote
is required for adoption.

to serve this year.
Respectfully submitted,

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE ON
TRIAL REVIEW
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The Committee on Trial Review has reviewed each of the trials
numbered 2544, 2546, 2547, 2548, 2549, 2550, 2538, 2539, 2545, 2537, and
2543, and finds that in each case there was sufficient compliance with the
procedural requirements of our Constitution and Ordinances; that the findings
and conclusions are supported by the trial record; and, that there was no
miscarriage of justice.
Your Committee therefore submits and moves for adoption the
following resolution:

“Resolved that in each of the trials numbered above the
judgment and penalty imposed be and are ratified, affirmed
and approved.”

The Committee reviewed 6 cases, numbered 2540, 2559, 2558, 2541,
2542, and 2554, in which the members were expelled without trial pursuant to
CMC § 1404.820. Although each file was examined to assure compliance with
the Code, they were not subject to further review by the Committee.
The members of the Committee would like to express their gratitude
for the opportunity and privilege to serve this Most Worshipful Grand Lodge.
Respectfully submitted,
Richard A. Meyers, Chairman
David D. Goble
Thomas C. Letcher
Donald B. McClary

Maxwell S. Roth
Robert E. Scott
Donald R. Fretz, Consultant

RECOGNITION OF FOREIGN GRAND LODGES
The Grand Lodge of California recognizes all of the Grand Lodges in the United States
published in the 2009 List of Lodges Masonic, which includes all 50 states and the
District of Columbia. In addition, the Grand Lodge of California recognizes a number of
Prince Hall Grand Lodges and foreign Grand Lodges around the world. The Grand
Lodge of California recognizes some, but not all, of the foreign Grand Lodges in the
2009 List of Lodges Masonic as well as a number of foreign Grand Lodges that are not
listed therein.
For clarity, the foreign Grand Lodges that are recognized by the Grand Lodge of
California are listed below alphabetically by country. For those that are published in the
2009 List of Lodges Masonic, the corresponding page number is provided in parenthesis.
Recognized Grand Lodges that are not published in the 2009 List of Lodges Masonic are
marked with an asterisk. The Grand Secretary of the Grand Lodge of California is
responsible for all correspondence with these Grand Lodges and the Lodges under their
jurisdiction. Please contact his office for assistance.

PRINCE HALL GRAND LODGES
The Grand Lodge of California has mutual recognition with a number of Prince Hall
Grand Lodges in the United States. In addition, the Grand Lodge of California has
extended an offer of recognition to a number of Prince Hall Grand Lodges throughout the
country where mutual recognition does not yet exist.
The Grand Lodge of California maintains mutual recognition with following Prince Hall
Grand Lodges. Some of these Grand Lodges are published in the List of Lodges Masonic
so the same markings follow the name of the Grand Lodge to indicate whether or not the
Grand Lodge is published in the List of Lodges Masonic, and if so, the 2009 edition page
number is provided. The terms of recognition are the same with each Prince Hall Grand
Lodge and are consistent with the agreement of mutual recognition between the Grand
Lodge of California and the Prince Hall Grand Lodge of California, Inc. In essence, dual
membership in Lodges under the two jurisdictions is not allowed. Visitation may be had
between members of Lodges of both jurisdictions. For these purposes, visitation means
visiting tiled meetings.
CALIFORNIA: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of California, Inc. (26); DISTRICT OF
COLUMBIA: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of the District of Columbia* (includes Lodges
in Maryland); MASSACHUSETTS: Prince Hall Grand Lodge Jurisdiction of
Massachusetts* (includes Lodges in New Hampshire and Maine); MISSOURI: Prince
Hall Grand Lodge of Missouri and Jurisdiction*; NEBRASKA: Prince Hall Grand
Lodge of Nebraska and Its Jurisdiction*; NEW MEXICO: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of
the State of New Mexico, Inc.*; OREGON: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Oregon*;
PENNSYLVANIA: Prince Hall Grand Lodge Jurisdiction of Pennsylvania* (includes
Lodges in Ohio).

The Grand Lodge of California has extended an offer of mutual recognition with the
following Prince Hall Grand Lodges, but mutual recognition has not yet been established.
Visitation between our members and members of Lodges under the jurisdiction of these
Grand Lodges can not yet be made. When mutual recognition is established, the Grand
Secretary will notify our Lodges in writing.
ALASKA: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Alaska and Its Jurisdiction, Inc.*; ARIZONA:
Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Arizona and Its Jurisdiction*; COLORADO (WYOMING
& UTAH): Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Colorado, Wyoming, and Utah*;
CONNECTICUT: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Connecticut, Inc. (29); HAWAII:
Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Hawaii and Its Jurisdiction*; ILLINOIS: Prince Hall Grand
Lodge of Illinois INDIANA: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Indiana, Inc.*; IOWA: Prince
Hall Grand Lodge of Iowa and Jurisdiction*; KANSAS: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of
Kansas and Its Jurisdiction*; MARYLAND: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Maryland and
Its Jurisdiction*; MICHIGAN: Prince Hall Grand Lodge of the Jurisdiction of
Michigan*; MINNESOTA: Prince Hall Grand Lodge Jurisdiction of Minnesota, Inc.*;
